  PROPERTY (T ) FOR GROUPS GRADED BY ROOT SYSTEMS


      MIKHAIL ERSHOV, ANDREI JAIKIN-ZAPIRAIN, AND MARTIN KASSABOV


         Abstract. We introduce and study the class of groups graded by root sys-
         tems. We prove that if Φ is an irreducible classical root system of rank ≥ 2 and
         G is a group graded by Φ, then under certain natural conditions on the grad-
         ing, the union of the root subgroups is a Kazhdan subset of G. As the main
         application of this theorem we prove that for any reduced irreducible classical
         root system Φ of rank ≥ 2 and a finitely generated commutative ring R with 1,
         the Steinberg group StΦ (R) and the elementary Chevalley group EΦ (R) have
         property (T ). We also show that there exists a group with property (T ) which
         maps onto all finite simple groups of Lie type and rank ≥ 2, thereby providing
         a “unified” proof of expansion in these groups.

                                1. Introduction
1.1. The main result. In this paper by a ring we will always mean an associative
ring with 1. In a recent work of the first two authors [EJ] it was shown that for any
integer n ≥ 3 and a finitely generated ring R, the elementary linear group ELn (R)
and the Steinberg group Stn (R) have Kazhdan’s property (T ) (in fact property (T )
for ELn (R) is a consequence of property (T ) for Stn (R) since ELn (R) is a quotient
                PROPERTY (T ) FOR GROUPS GRADED BY ROOT SYSTEMS                                3


of Stn (R)). In this paper we extend this result to elementary Chevalley groups and
Steinberg groups corresponding to other classical root systems of rank ≥ 2 (see
Theorem 1.1 below).
   We will use the term root system in a very broad sense (see § 4). By a classical
root system we mean the root system of some semisimple algebraic group (such
root systems are often called crystallographic).
   If Φ is a reduced irreducible classical root system and R a commutative ring,
denote by GΦ (R) the corresponding simply-connected Chevalley group over R and
by EΦ (R) the elementary subgroup of GΦ (R), that is, the subgroup generated by
the root subgroups with respect to the standard torus. For instance, if Φ = An−1 ,
then GΦ (R) = SLn (R) and EΦ (R) = ELn (R). For brevity we will refer to EΦ (R) as
an elementary Chevalley group. There is a natural epimorphism from the Steinberg
group StΦ (R) onto EΦ (R).
Theorem 1.1. Let Φ be a reduced irreducible classical root system of rank ≥ 2.
Let R be a finitely generated ring, which is commutative if Φ is not of type An .
Then the Steinberg group StΦ (R) and the elementary Chevalley group EΦ (R) have
Kazhdan’s property (T ).
Remark. There are many cases when EΦ (R) = GΦ (R). For instance, this holds
if R = Z[x1 , . . . , xk ] or R = F [x1 , . . . , xk ], where F is a field, and Φ is of type An
(see [Su]) or Cn (see [GMV]), with n ≥ 2.

   Steinberg groups and elementary Chevalley groups over rings are typical exam-
ples of groups graded by root systems which are introduced and studied in this paper.
Our central result asserts that if G is any group graded by a (finite) root system Φ
of rank ≥ 2, the grading satisfies certain non-degeneracy condition, and {Xα }α∈Φ
are the root subgroups, then ∪Xα is a Kazhdan subset of G (see Theorem 1.2 be-
low). Theorem 1.1 follows primarily from this result and relative property (T ) for
the pair (St2 (R) n R2 , R2 ) established by Shalom when R is commutative [Sh1]
and by the third author for general R [Ka1]; however, additional considerations are
needed in the case when Φ is not simply laced. Before discussing the proofs of these
results, we briefly comment on the previous work on property (T ) for Chevalley and
Steinberg groups and the proof of the main theorem in [EJ].
1.2. Property (T ) for ELn (R): summary of prior work. By the 1967 foun-
dational paper of Kazhdan [Kazh] and the subsequent work of Vaserstein [Va], the
Chevalley groups GΦ (Z) = EΦ (Z) and GΦ (F [t]) = EΦ (F [t]), where F is a finite
field, have property (T ) for any reduced irreducible classical root system of rank
≥ 2. The question of whether the groups EΦ (R), with rk(Φ) ≥ 2 (in particular, the
groups ELn (R), n ≥ 3) have property (T ) for “larger” rings R remained completely
open for a long time.1
    In 2005, Kassabov and Nikolov [KN1] showed that the group ELn (R), n ≥ 3,
has property (τ ) (a certain weak form of property (T )) for any finitely generated
commutative ring R, which gave an indication that these groups might also have
property (T ). This indication was partially confirmed by Shalom in 2006 who
    1The situation in rank 1 is completely different. It is easy to see that EL (R) does not have
                                                                               2
(T ) whenever R (possibly noncommutative) surjects onto Z or F [t], with F a finite field. A more
delicate argument shows that EL2 (R) does not have (T ) for any infinite commutative ring R (with
1).
4        MIKHAIL ERSHOV, ANDREI JAIKIN-ZAPIRAIN, AND MARTIN KASSABOV


proved in [Sh2] that the groups ELn (R) have property (T ) whenever R is commu-
tative and n ≥ Kdim(R)+2 (where Kdim(R) is the Krull dimension of R). In 2007,
Vaserstein [Va2] eliminated this restriction on the Krull dimension by showing that
ELn (R), n ≥ 3, has property (T ) for any finitely generated commutative ring R.
Finally, in [EJ] the result was extended to arbitrary finitely generated (associative)
rings, and the method of proof was very different from the one used by Shalom
and Vaserstein. To explain the idea behind this method, we recall some standard
terminology.
   Let G be a discrete group and S a subset of G. Following the terminology
in [BHV], we will say that S is a Kazhdan subset of G if every unitary representation
of G containing almost S-invariant vectors must contain a G-invariant vector. By
definition, G has property (T ) if it has a finite Kazhdan subset; however, one can
prove that G has (T ) by finding an infinite Kazhdan subset K such that the pair
(G, K) has relative property (T ) (see § 2 for details).
   If G = ELn (R), where n ≥ 3 and R is a finitely generated ring, the afore-
mentioned results of Shalom and Kassabov yield relative property (T ) for the pair
(G, X) where X = ∪i6=j Eij (R) is the union of root subgroups. Thus, establishing
property (T ) for G is reduced to showing that X is a Kazhdan subset. An easy
way to prove the latter is to show that G is boundedly generated by X — this is
the so-called bounded generation method of Shalom [Sh1]. However, G is known
to be boundedly generated by X only in a few cases, namely, when R is a finite
extension of Z or F [t], with F a finite field. In [EJ], it was proved that X is a
Kazhdan subset of G = ELn (R) for any ring R using a different method, described
in the next subsection.

1.3. Almost orthogonality, codistance and a spectral criterion from [EJ].
Suppose we are given a group G and a finite collection of subgroups H1 , . . . , Hk
which generate G, and we want to know whether the union of these subgroups
X = ∪Hi is a Kazhdan subset of G. By definition, this will happen if and only if
given a unitary representation V of G without (nonzero) invariant vectors, a unit
vector v ∈ V cannot be arbitrarily close to each of the subspaces V Hi (where as
usual V K denotes the subspace of K-invariant vectors). In the simplest case k = 2
the latter property is equivalent to asserting that the angle between subspaces V H1
and V H2 must be bounded away from 0. For an arbitrary k, the “closeness” between
the subspaces V1 , . . . , Vk of a Hilbert space V can be measured using the notion
of a codistance introduced in [EJ]. We postpone the formal definition until § 2;
here we just say that the codistance between {Vi }, denoted by codist(V1 , . . . , Vk ) is
a real number in the interval [1/k, 1], and in the above setting ∪Hi is a Kazhdan
subset of G if and only if sup{codist(V H1 , . . . , V Hk )} < 1 where V runs over all
representations of G with V G = {1}.
   Let H and K be subgroups of the same group. We define ^(H, K), the angle
between H and K, to be the infimum of the set {^(V H , V K )} where V runs over
all representations of hH, Ki without invariant vectors; we shall also say that H
and K are ε-orthogonal if cos ^(H, K) ≤ ε. The idea of using such angles to prove
property (T ) already appears in 1991 paper of Burger [Bu] and is probably implicit
in earlier works on unitary representations. However, this idea has not been fully
exploited until the paper of Dymara and Januszkiewicz [DJ] which shows that for
a group G generated by k subgroups H1 , . . . , Hk , property (T ) can be established
by controlling “local information”, the angles between Hi and Hj . More precisely,
              PROPERTY (T ) FOR GROUPS GRADED BY ROOT SYSTEMS                           5


in [DJ] it is proved that if Hi and Hj are ε-orthogonal for i 6= j for sufficiently small
ε, then ∪Hi is a Kazhdan subset of G (so if in addition each Hi is finite, then G has
property (T )). This “almost orthogonality method” was generalized in [EJ] using
the notion of codistance. As a result, a new spectral criterion for property (T ) was
obtained, which is applicable to groups with a given graph of groups decomposition,
as defined below.
   Let G be a group and Γ a finite graph. A decomposition of G over Γ is a choice
of a vertex subgroup Gν ⊆ G for each vertex ν of Γ and an edge subgroup Ge ⊆ G
for each edge e of Γ such that
     (i) G is generated by the vertex subgroups {Gν }
    (ii) Each vertex group Gν is generated by edge subgroups {Ge }, with e incident
         to ν
   (iii) If an edge e connects ν and ν 0 , then Ge is contained in Gν ∩ Gν 0 .
The spectral criterion [EJ, Theorem 5.1] asserts that if a group G has a decom-
position ({Gν }, {Ge }) over a graph Γ, and for each vertex ν of Γ the codistance
between subgroups {Ge }, with e incident to ν, is sufficiently small with respect to
the spectral gap of Γ, then the codistance between the vertex subgroups {Gν } is
less than 1 (and thus the union of vertex subgroups is a Kazhdan subset of G).

1.4. Groups graded by root systems and associated graphs of groups.
Property (T ) for the groups GΦ (R) and StΦ (R) will be proved using certain gen-
eralization of the spectral criterion from [EJ]. First we shall describe the relevant
graph decompositions of those groups, for simplicity concentrating on the case
G = ELn (R).
    For each n ≥ 2 consider the following graph denoted by Γ(An ). The ver-
tices of Γ(An ) are labeled by the elements of the symmetric group Sym(n + 1),
and two vertices σ and σ 0 are connected if any only if they are not opposite to
each other in the Cayley graph of Sym(n + 1) with respect to the generating set
{(12), (23), . . . , (n, n + 1)}.
    Now let R be ring and n ≥ 3. The group G = ELn (R) has a natural decom-
position over Γ(An−1 ) defined as follows. For each σ ∈ Sym(n) the vertex group
Gσ is defined to be the subgroup of G generated by {Xij : σ(i) < σ(j)} where
Xij = {eij (r) : r ∈ R}. Thus, vertex subgroups are precisely the maximal unipo-
tent subgroups of G normalized by the diagonal subgroup; in particular, the vertex
subgroup corresponding to the identity permutation is the upper-unitriangular sub-
group of ELn (R). If e is the edge connecting vertices σ and σ 0 , we define the edge
subgroup Ge to be the intersection Gσ ∩Gσ0 (note that this intersection is non-trivial
precisely when σ and σ 0 are connected in Γ(An−1 )).
    As already discussed in the last paragraph of § 1.2, property (T ) for G = ELn (R)
is reduced to showing that ∪Xij is a Kazhdan subset of G. By the standard bounded
generation argument [Sh1] it suffices to show that the larger subset ∪σ∈Sym(n) Gσ
(the union of vertex subgroups in the above decomposition) is Kazhdan, and one
might attempt to prove the latter by applying the spectral criterion from [EJ] to
the decomposition of G over Γ(An−1 ) described above. This almost works. More
precisely, the attempted application of this criterion yields a “boundary case”,
where equality holds in the place of the desired inequality. In order to resolve this
problem, a slightly generalized version of the spectral criterion must be used. The
precise conditions entering this generalized spectral criterion are rather technical
6       MIKHAIL ERSHOV, ANDREI JAIKIN-ZAPIRAIN, AND MARTIN KASSABOV


(see § 3), but these conditions are consequences of a very transparent property
satisfied by ELn (R), namely the fact that ELn (R) is strongly graded by a root
system of type An−1 (which has rank ≥ 2) as defined below.
   Let G be a group and Φ a classical root system. Suppose that G has a family of
subgroups {Xα }α∈Φ such that
                                            Y
(1.1)                    [Xα , Xβ ] ⊆                Xγ
                                      γ∈Φ∩(Z>0 α⊕Z>0 β)

for any α, β ∈ Φ such that α 6= −λβ with λ ∈ R>0 . Then we will say that G is
graded by Φ and {Xα } is a Φ-grading of G.
   Clearly, for any root system Φ the Steinberg group StΦ (R) and the elementary
Chevalley group EΦ (R) are graded by Φ (with {Xα } being the root subgroups). On
the other hand, any group G graded by Φ has a natural graph decomposition. We
already discussed how to do this for Φ = An (in which case the underlying graph
is Γ(An ) defined above). For an arbitrary Φ, the vertices of the underlying graph
Γ(Φ) are labeled by the elements of WΦ , the Coxeter group of type Φ, and given
w ∈ WΦ , the vertex subgroup Gw is defined to be hXα : wα ∈ Φ+ i where Φ+ is the
set of positive roots in Φ (with respect to some fixed choice of simple roots). The
edges of Γ(Φ) and the edge subgroups of G are defined as in the case Φ = An .
   Once again, the above decomposition of G over Γ(Φ) corresponds to the boundary
case of the spectral criterion from [EJ], and the generalized spectral criterion turns
out to be applicable under the addition assumption that the grading of G by Φ
is strong. Informally a grading is strong if the inclusion in (1.1) is not too far
from being an equality (see § 4 for precise definition). For instance, if Φ is a
simply-laced system, a sufficient condition for a Φ-grading {Xα } to be strong is
that [Xα , Xβ ] = Xα+β whenever α + β is a root.
   We can now formulate the central result of this paper.
Theorem 1.2. Let Φ be an irreducible classical root system of rank ≥ 2, and let G
be a group which admits a strong Φ-grading {Xα }. Then ∪Xα is a Kazhdan subset
of G.
   Theorem 1.2 in the case Φ = An was already established in [EJ]; however, this
was achieved by only considering the graph Γ(A2 ), called the magic graph on six
vertices in [EJ]. This was possible thanks to an observation that every group
strongly graded by An−1 , n ≥ 3. must also be strongly graded by A2 ; for simplicity
we illustrate the latter for G = ELn (R). If n = 3k, the A2 grading follows from the
well-known isomorphism EL3k (R) ∼    = EL3 (Matk (R)), and for arbitrary n one should
think of n × n matrices as “3 × 3 block-matrices with blocks of uneven size.” This
observation is a special case of the important concept of a reduction of root systems
discussed in the next subsection (see § 6 for full details).
   The proof of Theorem 1.2 for arbitrary Φ follows the same general approach as in
the case Φ = A2 done in [EJ], although some arguments which are straightforward
for Φ = A2 require delicate considerations in the general case. Perhaps more
importantly, the proof presented in this paper provides a conceptual explanation
of certain parts of the argument from [EJ] and shows that there was really nothing
“magic” about the graph Γ(A2 ).

1.5. Further examples. So far we have discussed important, but very specific
examples of groups graded by root systems – Chevalley and Steinberg groups. We
              PROPERTY (T ) FOR GROUPS GRADED BY ROOT SYSTEMS                           7


shall now describe two general methods of constructing new groups graded by root
systems. Thanks to Theorem 1.2 and suitable results on relative property (T ), in
this way we will also obtain new examples of Kazhdan groups.
    The first method is simply an adaptation of the construction of twisted Chevalley
groups to a slightly different setting. Suppose we are given a group G together with
a grading {Xα } by a root system Φ and a finite group Q of automorphisms of G
which permutes the root subgroups between themselves. Under some additional
conditions we can use this data to construct a new group graded by a (different)
root system. Without going into details, we shall mention that the new group,
denoted by G d Q , surjects onto certain subgroup of GQ , the group of Q-fixed points of

G, and the new root system often coincides with the set of orbits under the induced
action of Q on the original root system Φ. As a special case of this construction, we
can take G = StΦ (R) for some ring R and let Q be the cyclic subgroup generated
by an automorphism of G of the form dσ where σ is a ring automorphism of StΦ (R)
and d is a diagram automorphism of StΦ (R) having the same order as σ.
    In this way we will obtain “Steinberg covers” of the usual twisted Chevalley
groups over commutative rings of type 2 An , 2 Dn , 3 D4 and 2 E6 . The Steinberg cov-
ers for the groups of type 2 An (which are unitary groups over rings with involution)
can also be defined over non-commutative rings; moreover, the construction allows
additional variations leading to a class of groups known as hyperbolic unitary Stein-
berg groups (see [HO], [Bak2]). Using this method one can also construct interesting
families of groups which do not seem to have direct counterparts in the classical
theory of algebraic groups; for instance, we will define Steinberg groups of type
2
  F4 – these correspond to certain groups constructed by Tits [Ti] which, in turn,
generalize twisted Chevalley groups of type 2 F4 . We will show that among these
Steinberg-type groups the ones graded by a root system of rank ≥ 2 have property
(T ) under some natural finiteness conditions on the data used to construct the
twisted group.
    The second method is based on the notion of a reduction of root systems defined
below. This method does not directly produce new groups graded by root systems,
but rather shows how given a group G graded by a root system Φ, one can construct
a new grading of G by another root system of smaller rank.
    If Φ and Ψ are two root systems, a reduction of Φ to Ψ is just a surjective map
η : Φ → Ψ ∪ {0} which extends to a linear map between the real vector spaces
spanned by Φ and Ψ, respectively. Now if G is a group with a Φ-grading Xα ,
for each β ∈ Ψ we set Yβ = hXα : η(α) = βi. If the groups {Yβ }β∈Ψ happen to
generate G (which is automatic, for instance, if η does not map any roots to 0), it
is easy to see that {Yβ } is a Ψ-grading of G. Furthermore, if the original Φ-grading
was strong, then under some natural assumptions on the reduction η the new Ψ-
grading will also be strong (reductions with this property will be called 2-good).
We will show that every classical root system of rank ≥ 2 has a 2-good reduction
to a classical root system of rank 2 (that is, A2 , B2 , BC2 or G2 ).2 In this way we
reduce the proof of Theorem 1.1 to Theorem 1.2 for classical root systems of rank
2. While Theorem 1.2 is not any easier to prove in this special case, using such
reductions we obtain much better Kazhdan constants for the groups StΦ (R) and
EΦ (R) than what we are able to deduce from direct application of Theorem 1.2.
   2The reduction of A to A was already implicitly used in the proof of property (T ) for
                      n    2
Stn+1 (R) in [EJ].
8        MIKHAIL ERSHOV, ANDREI JAIKIN-ZAPIRAIN, AND MARTIN KASSABOV


   So far our discussion was limited to groups graded by classical root systems, but
our definition of Φ-grading makes sense for any finite subset Φ. In this paper by
a root system we mean any finite subset of Rn symmetric about the origin, and
Theorem 1.2 remains true for groups graded by any root system satisfying a minor
technical condition (these will be called regular root systems). There are plenty
of regular root systems, which are not classical, but there is no easy recipe for
constructing interesting groups graded by them. What we know is that reductions
can be used to obtain some exotic gradings on familiar groups – for instance, the
groups Stn (R) and ELn (R) naturally graded by An−1 can also be strongly graded
by certain two-dimensional root system In (see the end of § 6). We believe that the
analysis of this and other similar gradings can be used to construct new interesting
groups, but we leave such an investigation for a later project.

1.6. Application to expanders. The subject of expansion in finite simple groups
has seen a burst of activity over the past decade. One of the fundamental results in
this area, established in a combination of several papers [Ka2, KLN, BGT], asserts
that all (non-abelian) finite simple groups form a family of expanders (formally this
means that the Cayley graphs of those groups with respect to certain generating sets
of uniformly bounded size form a family of expanders). Note that proofs for different
families of finite simple groups use different techniques and also vary a lot in level of
complexity – for instance, the proof in the case of alternating groups [Ka2] is very
involved, while expansion for the groups SLn (Fp ), n ≥ 3 is merely a consequence
of the fact that SLn (Z) has property (T ).
    In this paper we address the question of which families F of finite simple groups
can be realized as quotients of a single group G with property (T ). We will refer
to a group which surjects onto every group in a family F as a mother group for
F. The main result of § 9 (see Theorem 9.3) asserts that the collection of all finite
simple groups of Lie type and rank ≥ 2 admits a mother group with property (T )
(thereby providing a unified proof of expansion for these groups). It is known that
this result cannot be extended to all finite simple groups (even those of Lie type)
since the family {SL2 (Fp )} does not have a mother group with (T ); however, it is
still possible that all finite simple groups have a mother group with property (τ )
(which would be sufficient for expansion). To prove Theorem 9.3 we first divide all
finite simple groups of Lie type and rank ≥ 2 into finitely many subfamilies. Then
for each subfamily F we construct a strong Ψ-grading for each group G ∈ F by a
suitable classical root system Ψ (depending only on F). Finally we show that all
groups in F are quotients of a (possibly twisted) Steinberg group associated to Ψ,
which has property (T ) by one of the criteria established in § 7 or 8.
    Concluding remarks. To the best of our knowledge, the notion of a group
graded by a root system (as defined in this paper) did not previously appear in the
literature, but closely related classes of groups were considered by several authors.
These include groups with Steinberg relations studied by Faulkner [Fa2] and groups
graded by root systems in the sense of Shi [Shi]. The latter class can be defined as
groups which are graded by classical root systems in our sense and endowed with
a suitable action of the corresponding Weyl group. These groups are investigated
further by Zhang (work in preparation) where they are called root graded groups.
A more general class of groups which includes groups graded by Kac-Moody root
systems has been studied in [LN1, LN2].
              PROPERTY (T ) FOR GROUPS GRADED BY ROOT SYSTEMS                          9


   After this paper was completed, Mimura [Mi] found a new proof of property (T )
as well as its generalization property (F Lp ) for the groups ELn (R) and Stn (R),
n ≥ 4, and it seems that his criterion should be applicable to the majority of
Chevalley and Steinberg groups considered in this paper. However, the method of
[Mi] cannot be used to effectively estimate Kazhdan constants.


                                 2. Preliminaries
   In this section we shall define the notions of property (T ), relative property (T ),
Kazhdan constants and Kazhdan ratios, orthogonality constants, angles and codis-
tances between subspaces of Hilbert spaces, and recall some basic facts about them.
We shall also state two new results on Kazhdan constants for nilpotent groups and
group extensions, which will be established at the end of the paper (in § 10).
   All groups in this paper will be assumed discrete, and we shall consider their
unitary representations on Hilbert spaces. By a subspace of a Hilbert space we shall
mean a closed subspace unless indicated otherwise.

2.1. Property (T ).

Definition. Let G be a group and S a subset of G.
    (a) Let V be a unitary representation of G. A nonzero vector v ∈ V is called
        (S, ε)-invariant if

                           ksv − vk ≤ εkvk for any s ∈ S.

    (b) Let V be a unitary representation of G without nonzero invariant vectors.
        The Kazhdan constant κ(G, S, V ) is the infimum of the set

                   {ε > 0 : V contains an (S, ε)-invariant vector}.

    (c) The Kazhdan constant κ(G, S) of G with respect to S is the infimum of the
        set {κ(G, S, V )} where V runs over all unitary representations of G without
        nonzero invariant vectors.
    (d) S is called a Kazhdan subset of G if κ(G, S) > 0.
    (e) A group G has property (T ) if G has a finite Kazhdan subset, that is, if
        κ(G, S) > 0 for some finite subset S of G.

Remark. The Kazhdan constant κ(G, S) may only be nonzero if S is a generat-
ing set for G (see, e.g., [BHV, Proposition 1.3.2]). Thus, a group with property
(T ) is automatically finitely generated. Furthermore, if G has property (T ), then
κ(G, S) > 0 for any finite generating set S of G, but the Kazhdan constant κ(G, S)
depends on S.

   We note that if S is a “large” subset of G, positivity of the Kazhdan constant
κ(G, S) does not tell much about the group G. In particular, the following holds
(see, e.g., [BHV, Proposition 1.1.5] or [Sh1, Lemma 2.5] for a slightly weaker ver-
sion):
                                                   √
Lemma 2.1. For any group G we have κ(G, G) ≥ 2.
10        MIKHAIL ERSHOV, ANDREI JAIKIN-ZAPIRAIN, AND MARTIN KASSABOV


2.2. Relative property (T ) and Kazhdan ratios. Relative property (T ) has
been originally defined for pairs (G, H) where H is a normal subgroup of G:
Definition. Let G be a group and H a normal subgroup of G. The pair (G, H)
has relative property (T ) if there exist a finite set S and ε > 0 such that if V is any
unitary representation of G with an (S, ε)-invariant vector, then V has a (nonzero)
H-invariant vector. The largest ε with this property (for a fixed set S) is called the
relative Kazhdan constant of (G, H) with respect to S and denoted by κ(G, H; S).
Remark. In the computation of relative Kazhdan constants it is enough to consider
cyclic representations of G. Indeed, if V is any unitary representation of G which
does not have H-invariant vectors, but has an (S, ε)-invariant vector v, then W :=
span(Gv), the cyclic subrepresentation generated by v, has the same property. Of
course, the same remark applies to the computation of usual Kazhdan constants.
   The generalization of the notion of relative property (T ) to pairs (G, B), where
B is an arbitrary subset of a group G, has been given by de Cornulier [Co] and can
be defined as follows (see also a remark in [EJ, § 2]):
Definition. Let G be a group and B a subset of G. The pair (G, B) has relative
property (T ) if for any ε > 0 there are a finite subset S of G and µ > 0 such that
if V is any unitary representation of G and v ∈ V is (S, µ)-invariant, then v is
(B, ε)-invariant.
   In this more general setting it is not clear how to “quantify” the relative property
(T ) using a single real number. However, this is possible under the additional
assumption that the dependence of µ on ε in the above definition may be expressed
by a linear function. In this case we can define the notion of a Kazhdan ratio:
Definition. Let G be a group and B and S subsets of G. The Kazhdan ratio
κr (G, B; S) is the largest δ ∈ R with the following property: if V is any unitary
representation of G and v ∈ V is (S, δε)-invariant, then v is (B, ε)-invariant.
  Somewhat surprisingly, there is a simple relationship between Kazhdan ratios
and relative Kazhdan constants:
Observation 2.2. Let G be a group, and let B and S be subsets of G. The following
hold:
     (i) If κr (G, B; S) > 0 and S is finite, then (G, B) has relative (T ).
    (ii) If B is a normal subgroup of G, then
                      √
                        2κr (G, B; S) ≤ κ(G, B; S) ≤ 2κr (G, B; S).
           In particular, (G, B) has relative (T ) if and only if κr (G, B; S) > 0 for
           some finite set S.
     (iii) κ(G, S) ≥ κ(G, B)κr (G, B; S)
Proof. (i) and (iii) follow immediately from the definition. The first inequality in
(ii) holds by Lemma 2.1 applied to B (here we just need B to be a subgroup,
not necessarily normal). Finally, the second inequality in (ii) is a standard fact
proved, for instance, in [Sh1, Corollary 2.3]), but for completeness we reproduce
the argument here.
    Consider any unitary representation V of G. We have V = V B ⊕ (V B )⊥ , where
both V B and (V B )⊥ are G-invariant since B is normal in G. Now take any nonzero
                PROPERTY (T ) FOR GROUPS GRADED BY ROOT SYSTEMS                              11


v ∈ V , and assume that v is (S, δ)-invariant for some δ. Write v = vb + vb⊥ where
vb ∈ V B and vb⊥ ∈ (V B )⊥ .
   For any s ∈ S we have ksv − vk ≤ δkvk. Since both V B and (V B )⊥ are G-
invariant, ksv − vk2 = ksvb − vb k2 + ksvb⊥ − vb⊥ k2 , and thus
                                    ksvb⊥ − vb⊥ k ≤ δkvk.
On the other hand, (V B )⊥ has no B-invariant vectors, so there exists s ∈ S such
that ksvb⊥ − vb⊥ k ≥ µkvb⊥ k where µ = κ(G, B; S) (we can assume that µ > 0
since otherwise there is nothing to prove). Combining the two inequalities, we get
kvb⊥ k ≤ µδ kvk.
   Now take any b ∈ B. Since b fixes vb , we have
                                                                2δ
                       kbv − vk = kbvb⊥ − vb⊥ k ≤ 2kvb⊥ k ≤        kvk,
                                                                µ
so by definition of the Kazhdan ratio, κr (G, B; S) ≥ µ2 , as desired.                       

2.3. Using relative property (T ). A typical way to prove that a group G has
property (T ) is to find a subset K of such that
    (a) K is a Kazhdan subset of G
    (b) the pair (G, K) has relative property (T ).
   Clearly, (a) and (b) imply that G has property (T ). Note that (a) is easy to
establish when K is a large subset of G, while (b) is easy to establish when K is
small, so to obtain (a) and (b) simultaneously one typically needs to pick K of
intermediate size.
   In all our examples, pairs with relative property (T ) will be produced with the
aid of the following fundamental result: if R is any finitely generated ring, then
the pair (EL2 (R) n R2 , R2 ) has relative property (T ). This has been proved by
Burger [Bu] for R = Z, by Shalom [Sh1] for commutative R and by Kassabov [Ka1]
in general. In fact, we shall use what formally is a stronger result, although its
proof is identical to the one given in [Ka1]:
Theorem 2.3. Let R ? R denote the free product of two copies of the additive group
of R, and consider the semi-direct product (R ? R) n R2 , where the first copy of R
acts by 
        upper-unitriangular
                           matrices, that is, r ∈ R acts as left multiplication by the
          1 r
matrix            , and the second copy of R acts by lower-unitriangular matrices.
          0 1
Then the pair ((R ? R) n R2 , R2 ) has relative property (T ).
   In § 7 we shall state a higher-dimensional generalization of this theorem along
with an explicit bound for the relative Kazhdan constant (see Theorem 7.10). For
completeness, we will present proofs of both Theorem 2.3 and 7.10 in Appendix A.
   Given a group G, once we have found some subsets B of G for which (G, B)
has relative property (T ), it is easy to produce many more subsets with the same
property. First it is clear that if (G, Bi ) has relative (T ) for some finite collection of
subsets B1 , . . . , Bk , then (G, ∪Bi ) also has relative (T ). Indeed, suppose that given
ε > 0 there exist finite subsets S1 , . . . , Sk of G and µ1 , . . . , µk > 0 such that for each
1 ≤ i ≤ k, if a vector v in some unitary representation of G is (Si , µi )-invariant,
then v is also (Bi , ε)-invariant. If we set S = ∪Si and µ = min{µi }, then any v
which is (S, µ)-invariant must also be (B, ε)-invariant.
12          MIKHAIL ERSHOV, ANDREI JAIKIN-ZAPIRAIN, AND MARTIN KASSABOV

method is based.

elements of G representable as b1 . . . bk with bi ∈ Bi .

        has relative (T ).
    (b) Suppose in addition that κr (G, Bi ; S) > 0 for each i. Then

     For convenience, we introduce the following terminology:
Definition. Let G be a group and B1 , . . . , Bk a finite collection of subsets of G.
Let H be another subset of G. We will say that H lies in a bounded product of
B1 , . . . , Bk if there exists N ∈ N such that any element h ∈ H can be written as
h = h1 . . . hN with hi ∈ ∪kj=1 Bj for all i.
   By Lemma 2.4 if a group G has subsets B1 , . . . , Bk such that (G, Bi ) has relative
(T ) for each i, then (G, H) has relative (T ) for any subset H which lies in a bounded
product of B1 , . . . , Bk . This observation will be frequently used in the proof of
property (T ) for groups graded by non-simply laced root systems.
2.4. Orthogonality constants, angles and codistances. The notion of the
orthogonality constant between two subspaces of a Hilbert space was introduced
and successfully applied in [DJ] and also played a key role in [EJ]:
Definition. Let V1 and V2 be two (closed) subspaces of a Hilbert space V .
    (i) The orthogonality constant orth(V1 , V2 ) between V1 and V2 is defined by
               orth(V1 , V2 ) = sup{|hv1 , v2 i| : ||vi || = 1, vi ∈ Vi for i = 1, 2}
      (ii) The quantity ^(V1 , V2 ) = arccos(orth(V1 , V2 )) will be called the angle be-
           tween V1 and V2 . Thus, ^(V1 , V2 ) is the infimum of angles between a
           nonzero vector from V1 and a nonzero vector from V2 .
   These quantities are only of interest when the subspaces V1 and V2 intersect
trivially. In general, it is more adequate to consider the corresponding quantities
after factoring out the intersection. We call them the reduced orthogonality constant
and reduced angle.
Definition. Let V1 and V2 be two subspaces in a Hilbert space V , and assume that
neither of the subspaces V1 and V2 contains the other.
               PROPERTY (T ) FOR GROUPS GRADED BY ROOT SYSTEMS                                 13


    (i) The reduced orthogonality constant orthred (V1 , V2 ) between V1 and V2 is
        defined by
    orthred (V1 , V2 ) = sup{hv1 , v2 i | ||vi || = 1, vi ∈ Vi , vi ⊥ V1 ∩ V2 for i = 1, 2}.
   (ii) The quantity ^red (V1 , V2 ) = arccos(orthred (V1 , V2 )) will be called the re-
        duced angle between V1 and V2 . Thus, ^red (V1 , V2 ) is the infimum of the
        angles between nonzero vectors v1 and v2 , where vi ∈ Vi and vi ⊥ V1 ∩ V2 .
Remark. If V1 and V2 are two distinct planes in a three-dimensional Euclidean
space, then the reduced angle ^red (V1 , V2 ) coincides with the usual (geometric)
angle between V1 and V2 .
   Reduced angles play a key role in Kassabov’s paper [Ka3] (where they are called
just ‘angles’), but in the present paper the case of subspaces with trivial inter-
sections will suffice. In fact, in the subsequent discussion we shall operate with
orthogonality constants rather than angles.
   The following simple result will be very important for our purposes.
Lemma 2.5. Let V1 and V2 be two subspaces of a Hilbert space V . Then the reduced
angle between the orthogonal complements V1⊥ and V2⊥ is equal to the reduced angle
between V1 and V2 . Equivalently,
                           orthred (V1⊥ , V2⊥ ) = orthred (V1 , V2 ).
Proof. This result appears as [Ka3, Lemma 3.9] as well as [EJ, Lemma 2.4] (in a
special case), but it has apparently been known to functional analysts for a long
time (see [BGM] and references therein).                                       

   The notion of codistance introduced in [EJ] generalizes orthogonality constants
to the case of more than two subspaces.
Definition. Let V be a Hilbert space, and let {Ui }ni=1 be subspaces of V . Con-
sider the Hilbert space V n and its subspaces U1 × U2 × . . . × Un and diag(V ) =
{(v, v, . . . , v) : v ∈ V }. The quantity
                                                                          2
                    codist({Ui }) = (orth(U1 × . . . × Un , diag(V )))
will be called the codistance between the subspaces {Ui }ni=1 . It is easy to see that
                                ku1 + · · · + un k2
                                                                    X           
                                                                            2
      codist({Ui }) = sup                                : ui ∈ Ui ,   kui k 6= 0 .
                             n(ku1 k2 + · · · + kun k2 )
  For any collection of n subspaces {Ui }ni=1 we have n1 ≤ codist({Ui }) ≤ 1, and
codist({Ui }) = n1 if and only if {Ui } are pairwise orthogonal. In the case of two
subspaces we have an obvious relation between codist(U, W ) and orth(U, W ):
                                           1 + orth(U, W )

Similarly one can define the reduced codistance, but we shall not use this notion. The
closely related notion of (reduced) angle between several subspaces is investigated
in [Ka3].
   We now define the orthogonality constants and codistances for subgroups of a
given group.
14       MIKHAIL ERSHOV, ANDREI JAIKIN-ZAPIRAIN, AND MARTIN KASSABOV


Definition.      (a) Let H and K be subgroups of the same group and let G =

       supremum of the quantities orth(V H , V K ) where V runs over all unitary
       representations of G without nonzero invariant vectors.
   (b) Let {Hi }ni=1 be subgroups of the same group, and let G = hH1 , . . . , Hn i.
       The codistance between {Hi }, denoted by codist({Hi }), is defined to be
       the supremum of the quantities codist(V H1 , . . . , V Hn ), where V runs over
       all unitary representations of G without nonzero invariant vectors.
   The basic connection between codistance and property (T ), already discussed in
the introduction, is given by the following lemma (see [EJ, Lemma 3.1]):
Lemma 2.6 ([EJ]). Let G be a group and H1 , H2 , . . . , Hn subgroups of G such that
G = hH1 , . . . , Hn i. Let ρ = codist({Hi }), and suppose that ρ < 1. The following
hold:
                S        p

         G has property (T ).
2.5. Kazhdan constants for nilpotent groups and group extensions. We
finish this section by formulating two theorems and one simple lemma which provide
estimates for Kazhdan constants. These results are new (although they have been
known before in some special cases [BHV, Ha, NPS]). The two theorems will be
established in § 10 of this paper, while the lemma will be proved right away.
   The first theorem concerns relative Kazhdan constants in central extensions of
groups:
Theorem 2.7. Let G be a group, N a normal subgroup of G and Z a subgroup
of Z(G) ∩ N . Put H = Z ∩ [N, G]. Assume that A, B and C are subsets of G
satisfying the following conditions:
    (1) A and N generate G,
    (2) κ(G/Z, N/Z; B) ≥ ε,
    (3) κ(G/H, Z/H; C) ≥ δ.
Then                                      (                      )
                                     1             12ε
              κ(G, N ; A ∪ B ∪ C) ≥ √ min    p                 ,δ .
                                      3     5 72ε2 |A| + 25|B|
   In a typical application of this theorem the following additional conditions will
be satisfied:
    (a) The group G/N is finitely generated
    (b) The group Z/H is finite
In this case (3) holds automatically, and (1) holds for some finite set A. Therefore,
Theorem 2.7 implies that under the additional assumptions (a) and (b), relative
property (T ) for the pair (G/Z, N/Z) implies relative property (T ) for the pair
(G, N ).
   The second theorem that we shall use gives a bound for the codistance between
subgroups of a nilpotent group. It is not difficult to see that if G is an abelian
               PROPERTY (T ) FOR GROUPS GRADED BY ROOT SYSTEMS                          15


group generated by subgroups X1 , . . . , Xk , then codist(X1 , . . . , Xk ) ≤ 1 − k1 (and
this bound is optimal). We shall prove a similar (likely far from optimal) bound in
the case of countable nilpotent groups:
Theorem 2.8. Let G be a countable nilpotent group of class c generated by sub-
groups X1 , . . . , Xk . Then
                                                             1

and by e+ = y the terminal vertex of e.

3.2. Spectral criteria.
Definition. Let G be a group and Γ a finite graph without loops. A graph of
groups decomposition (or just a decompoisition) of G over Γ is a choice of a vertex
subgroup Gν ⊆ G for every ν ∈ V(Γ) and an edge subgroup Ge ⊆ G for every
e ∈ E(Γ) such that
    (a) The vertex subgroups {Gν : ν ∈ V(Γ)} generate G;
    (b) Ge = Gē and Ge ⊆ Ge+ ∩ Ge− for every e ∈ E(Γ).
We will say that the decomposition of G over Γ is regular if for each ν ∈ V(Γ) the
vertex group Gν is generated by the edge subgroups {Ge : e+ = ν}
     The following criterion is proved in [EJ]:

decomposition of Ω0 (Γ, V ) into a direct sum of three subspaces, and denote by
ρ1 , ρ2 , ρ3 the projection maps onto those subspaces.

Ω0 (Γ, V ) and ν ∈ V(Γ) we set
      ρ1 (g)(ν) = πV Gν (g(ν))            ρ2 (g)(ν) = π(V Gν )⊥ ∩V CGν (g(ν))
      ρ3 (g)(ν) = π(V CGν )⊥ (g(ν)),
that is, the values of ρi (g) for i = 1, 2 and 3 at the vertex ν are the projections
of the vector g(ν) ∈ V onto the subspaces V Gν , (V Gν )⊥ ∩ V CGν and (V CGν )⊥ ,
respectively.
   By construction ρ1 is just the projection onto Ω0 (Γ, V ){Gν } , and we have
          kρ1 (g)k2 + kρ2 (g)k2 + kρ3 (g)k2 = kgk2 for every g ∈ Ω0 (Γ, V ).
   Similarly, we define the projections ρ1 , ρ2 , ρ3 on the space Ω1 (Γ, V ): for a func-
tion g ∈ Ω1 (Γ, V ) and an edge e ∈ E(Γ) we set
     ρ1 (g)(e) = πV Ge+ (g(e))           ρ2 (g)(e) = π(V Ge+ )⊥ ∩V CGe+ (g(e))
     ρ3 (g)(e) = π(V CGe+ )⊥ (g(e)),
Again we have
          kρ1 (g)k2 + kρ2 (g)k2 + kρ3 (g)k2 = kgk2 for every g ∈ Ω1 (Γ, V ).
Claim 3.2. The projections ρi , i = 1, 2, 3, preserve the subspace Ω1 (Γ, V ){Ge } .
Proof. The projection ρ1 preserve the space Ω1 (Γ, V ){Ge } because Ge+ contains the
group Ge . The other two projections preserve this space because CGe+ is a normal
subgroup of Ge+ .                                                                 

                                   4. Root systems
   The definition of a root system used in this paper is much less restrictive than
that of a classical root system. However, most constructions associated with root
systems we shall consider are naturally motivated by the classical case.
Definition. Let E be a real vector space. A finite non-empty subset Φ of E is
called a root system in E if
    (a) Φ spans E;
    (b) Φ does not contain 0;
     (c) Φ is closed under inversion, that is, if α ∈ Φ then −α ∈ Φ.
The dimension of E is called the rank of Φ.
Remark. Sometimes we shall refer to the pair (E, Φ) as a root system.
Definition. Let Φ be a root system in E.
    (i) Φ is called reduced if any line in E contains at most two elements of Φ;
   (ii) Φ is called irreducible if it cannot be represented as a disjoint union of two
        non-empty subsets, whose R-spans have trivial intersection.
  (iii) A subset Ψ of Φ is called a root subsystem of Φ if Ψ = Φ ∩ RΨ, where RΨ
        is the R-span of Ψ.
     The importance of the following definition will be explained later in this section.
Definition. A root system will be called regular if any root is contained in an
irreducible subsystem of rank 2.
4.1. Classical root systems. In this subsection we define classical root systems
and state some well-known facts about them. The reader is referred to [Bou, Ch.VI]
and [Hu1, Ch.III] for more details.
Definition. A root system Φ in a space E will be called classical if E can be
given the structure of a Euclidean space with inner product (·, ·) such that for any
α, β ∈ Φ
      (a) 2(α,β)
           (β,β) ∈ Z;
              PROPERTY (T ) FOR GROUPS GRADED BY ROOT SYSTEMS                          21


    (b) α − 2(α,β)
             (β,β) β ∈ Φ.

Any inner product on E satisfying (a) and (b) will be called admissible .

Fact 4.1.      (a) Every irreducible classical root system is isomorphic to one of
       the following: An , Bn (n ≥ 2), Cn (n ≥ 3), BCn (n ≥ 1), Dn (n ≥ 4), E6 ,
       E7 , E8 , F4 , G2 . The only non-reduced systems in this list are those of type
       BCn .
   (b) Every irreducible classical root system of rank ≥ 2 is regular.

Proof. (a) is well known (see, e.g., [Bou, VI.4.2, VI.4.14]), and (b) can be proved
by straightforward case-by-case verification.                                    

   If Φ is a classical irreducible root system in a space E, then an admissible in-
ner product (·, ·) on E is uniquely defined up to rescaling. In particular, we can
compare lengths of different roots in Φ without specifying the Euclidean structure.
Furthermore, the following hold:
     (i) If Φ = An , Dn , E6 , E7 or E8 , all roots in Φ have the same length;
    (ii) If Φ = Bn , Cn , F4 or G2 , there are two different root lengths in Φ;
   (iii) If Φ = BCn , there are three different root lengths in Φ.
   As usual, in case (ii), roots of smaller length will be called short and the remain-
ing ones called long . In case (iii) roots of smallest length will be called short, roots
of intermediate length called long and roots of largest length called double . The
latter terminology is due to the fact that double roots in BCn are precisely roots
of the form 2α where α is also a root.

Definition. A subset Π of a classical root system Φ is called a base (or a system
of simple roots) if every root in Φ is an integral linear combination of elements of
Π with all coefficients positive or all coefficients negative. Thus every base Π of
Φ determines a decomposition of Φ into two disjoint subsets Φ+ (Π) and Φ− (Π) =
−Φ+ (Π), called the sets of positive (resp. negative) roots with respect to Π.

Example 4.2. Figure 4.1 illustrates each irreducible classical root system of rank 2
with a chosen base Π = {α, β}.
        If Φ = A2 , then Φ+ (Π) = {α, β, α + β}
        If Φ = B2 , then Φ+ (Π) = {α, β, α + β, α + 2β}
        If Φ = BC2 , then Φ+ (Π) = {α, β, α + β, 2β, α + 2β, 2α + 2β}
        If Φ = G2 , then Φ+ (Π) = {α, β, α + β, α + 2β, α + 3β, 2α + 3β}

   Every classical root system Φ in a space E has a base; in fact, the number of
(unordered) bases is equal to the order of the Weyl group of Φ. If Π is a base of Φ,
it must be a basis of E. Observe that if f : E → R is any functional which takes
positive values on Π, then
                            Φ+ (Π) = {α ∈ Φ : f (α) > 0}.
   Conversely, if f : E → R is any functional which does not vanish on any of the
roots in Φ, one can show that the set Φf = {α ∈ Φ : f (α) > 0} coincides with
Φ+ (Π) for some base Π. In fact, Π can be characterized as the elements α ∈ Φf
which are not representable as β + γ, with β, γ ∈ Φf .
22            MIKHAIL ERSHOV, ANDREI JAIKIN-ZAPIRAIN, AND MARTIN KASSABOV

4.2. General root systems. In this subsection we extend the notions of a base
and a set of positive roots from classical to arbitrary root systems. By the discus-
sion at the end of the last subsection, if Φ is a classical root system, the sets of
positive roots with respect to different bases of Φ are precisely the Borel subsets
of Φ as defined below. The suitable generalization of the notion of a base, called
the boundary of a Borel set, is less straightforward and will be given later. The
terminology ‘Borel subset’ will be explained in §4.3.
Remark. If Φ is a reduced classical root system, the notions of boundary and base
for Borel subsets of Φ coincide. However, if Φ is not reduced, the boundary of a
Borel subset will be larger than its base.
Definition. Let Φ be a root system in a space E. Let F = F(Φ) denote the set of
all linear functionals f : E → R such that
     (1) f (α) 6= 0 for all α ∈ Φ;
     (2) f (α) 6= f (β) for any distinct α, β ∈ Φ.
For f ∈ F, the set Φf = {α ∈ Φ | f (α) > 0} is called the Borel set of f . The sets of
this form will be called Borel subsets of Φ. We will say that two elements f, f 0 ∈ F
are equivalent and write f ∼ f 0 if Φf = Φf 0 .
Remark. Note that condition (2) implies condition (1) (if f (α) = 0, then f (−α) =
f (α)), but we will not use this fact.
                                                       PROPERTY (T ) FOR GROUPS GRADED BY ROOT SYSTEMS                                                                                                             23

            Remark. Observe that for any f ∈ F we can order the elements in Φf as follows:
                                                                                      Φf = {αf,1 , αf,2 , . . . αf,k }
            where k = |Φf | = |Φ|/2 and
                                                                               f (αf,1 ) > f (αf,2 ) > · · · > f (αf,k ) > 0.
            If f and g are equivalent functionals, their Borel sets Φf and Φg coincide, but the
            orderings on Φf = Φg induced by f and g may be different.
               For instance, if Φ = A2 and {α, β} is a base of Φ, the functionals f and f 0 defined
            by f (α) = f 0 (α) = 2, f (β) = 1 and f 0 (β) = 3 define the same Borel set consisting
            of the roots α, β and α + β, however the ordering induced by f and f 0 are different
                                                      f (β) < f (α) < f (α + β)                        f 0 (α) < f 0 (β) < f 0 (α + β).
            Definition. Let Φ be a root system. Two Borel sets Φf and Φg will be called
                        • opposite if Φf ∩ Φg = ∅ or, equivalently, Φg = Φ−f ;
                        • co-maximal if an inclusion Φh ⊃ Φf ∩ Φg implies that Φh = Φf or Φh = Φg ;

                        • co-minimal if Φf and Φ−g are co-maximal.
            Example 4.3. Figure 4.2 shows the Borel sets in root systems of type A2 and B2 .
            Pairs of opposite Borel sets are connected with a dotted line and co-maximal ones
            are connected with a solid line.

            Lemma 4.4. Let Φ be a root system in a space E, and let Φf and Φg be distinct
            Borel sets. The following are equivalent:
                   (i) Φf ∩ Φ−g spans one-dimensional subspace
                  (ii) Φf and Φg are co-maximal
                 (iii) If h ∈ F is such that Φh ⊃ Φf ∩ Φg , then Φh ∩ Φg = Φf ∩ Φg or Φh ∩ Φf =
                       Φ f ∩ Φg .
24        MIKHAIL ERSHOV, ANDREI JAIKIN-ZAPIRAIN, AND MARTIN KASSABOV


Proof.    (i)⇒ (ii) Since Φf ∩ Φ−g spans one-dimensional subspace, there exists a
vector v ∈ E such that
                   Φf = (Φf ∩ Φg ) ∪ (Φf ∩ Φ−g ) ⊂ (Φf ∩ Φg ) ∪ R>0 v
and
                   Φg = (Φf ∩ Φg ) ∪ (Φ−f ∩ Φg ) ⊂ (Φf ∩ Φg ) ∪ R<0 v.
Thus, if Φh contains Φf ∩ Φg , then Φh = Φf in the case h(v) > 0 or Φh = Φg in
the case h(v) < 0. Hence Φf and Φg are co-maximal.
    (ii)⇒ (iii) is obvious.
    (iii)⇒ (i) Let U be the subspace spanned by Φf ∩Φ−g , and suppose that dim U >
1. Since any sufficiently small perturbation of f does not change its equivalence
class, we may assume that the the restrictions of f and g to U are linearly inde-
pendent.
    For any x ∈ [0, 1] consider hx = xf + (1 − x)g. Note that h0 = g is negative on
Φf ∩ Φ−g and h1 = f is positive on Φf ∩ Φ−g . Since the restrictions of f and g to
U are linearly independent, by continuity there exists x ∈ (0, 1) such that h = hx
is positive on some but not all roots from Φf ∩ Φ−g . Thus there exist α, β ∈ Φ
such that f (α) > 0, g(α) < 0, h(α) > 0 and f (β) > 0, g(β) < 0, h(β) < 0, so
Φf ∩Φg 6= Φf ∩Φh and Φf ∩Φg 6= Φh ∩Φg . On the the hand, since h = xf +(1−x)g,
it is clear that Φf ∩ Φg is contained in Φh . This contradicts (iii).
                                                                                  

     Part (a) of the next definition generalizes the notion of a base of a root system.
Definition.          (a) The boundary of a Borel set Φf is the set
               [               [
       ∂Φf =       (Φf \ Φg ) = (Φf ∩ Φ−g ), where Φg and Φf are co-maximal.
               g                  g

          Equivalently,
                            [
                ∂Φf = Φf ∩ ( Φg ), where Φg and Φf are co-minimal.
                               g

      (b) The core of a Borel set Φf is the set
                           \
          Cf = Φf \ ∂Φf = (Φf ∩ Φg ), where Φg and Φf are co-maximal.
                              g

   If Φ is a classical reduced system and Φf is a Borel subset of Φ, it is easy to see
that the boundary of Φf is precisely the base Π for which Φ+ (Π) = Φf . However
for non-reduced systems this is not the case and the boundary also contains all
roots which are positive multiples of the roots in the base.
Example 4.5. In each of the following examples we consider a classical rank 2 root
system Φ, its base Π = {α, β} and the Borel set Φ+ (Π).
     1. If Φ = A2 , the core of the Borel set {α, β, α+β} is {α+β} and the boundary
        is {α, β}.
     2. If Φ = B2 , the core of the Borel set {α, β, α + β, α + 2β} is {α + β, α + 2β}
        and the boundary is again {α, β}.
     3. If Φ = BC2 , the core of the Borel set {α, β, 2β, α + β, 2α + 2β, α + 2β} is
        {α + β, 2α + 2β, α + 2β}, while the boundary is {α, β, 2β}.
             PROPERTY (T ) FOR GROUPS GRADED BY ROOT SYSTEMS                     25


Lemma 4.6. Let Φ be a root system, f ∈ F = F(Φ), and let α, β ∈ Φf be linearly
independent. Then any root in Φ of the form aα + bβ, with a, b > 0, lies in Cf .
Proof. Assume the contrary, in which case aα + bβ ∈ ∂Φf . Thus, there exists
g ∈ F(Φ) such that Φf and Φg are co-minimal and aα + bβ ∈ Φf ∩ Φg . But then
g(β) > 0 or g(α) > 0, so Φf ∩ Φg contains linearly independent roots aα + bβ and
α or β. This contradicts Lemma 4.4.                                            
Lemma 4.7. Let Φ be a root system, Ψ a subsystem, f ∈ F = F(Φ) and f0 the
restriction of f to RΨ. Then the core Cf0 of Ψf0 is a subset of the core Cf of Φf .
Proof. Let v ∈ Cf0 . We have to show that for any g ∈ F such that Φf and
Φg are co-maximal, g(v) > 0. Assume the contrary, that is, g(v) < 0. Then
by Corollary 4.4, Φf ∩ Φ−g ⊂ Rv. Let g0 be the restriction of g on Ψ. Then
∅ 6= Ψf0 ∩ Ψ−g0 ⊆ Φf ∩ Φ−g ⊂ Rv. Again by Corollary 4.4, Ψf0 and Ψ−g0 are co-
minimal, so Ψf0 and Ψg0 are co-maximal. Since v ∈ Cf0 , we have g(v) = g0 (v) > 0,
a contradiction.                                                                
Lemma 4.8. Every root in an irreducible rank 2 system Φ is contained in the core
of some Borel set.
Proof. Let E be the vector space spanned by Φ, and take any α ∈ Φ. Since
Φ is irreducible, there exist β, γ ∈ Φ such that α, β and γ are pairwise linearly
independent. Replacing β by −β and γ by −γ if necessary, we can assume that
α = bβ + cγ with b, c > 0. If we now take any f ∈ F(Φ) such that f (β) > 0 and
f (γ) > 0, then α ∈ Cf by Lemma 4.6.                                           
Corollary 4.9. Every root in a regular root system is contained in the core of some
Borel set.
Proof. This follows from Lemmas 4.7 and 4.8 and the fact that if Ψ is a subsystem
of Φ, then any element of F(Ψ) is the restriction of some element of F(Φ) to RΨ. 
4.3. Weyl graphs. To each root system Φ we shall associate two graphs Γl (Φ)
and Γs (Φ), called the large Weyl graph and the small Weyl graph, respectively.
Both Weyl graphs Γl = Γl (Φ) and Γs = Γs (Φ) will have the same vertex set:
                           V(Γl ) = V(Γs ) = F(Φ)/ ∼ .
Thus vertices of either graph are naturally labeled by Borel subsets of Φ: to each
vertex f ∈ V(Γl ) = V(Γs ) we associate the Borel set Φf .
      • Two vertices f and g are connected in the large Weyl graph Γl if and only
        if their Borel sets are not opposite;
      • Two vertices f and g are connected in the small Weyl graph Γs if and
        only if there exists functionals f 0 and g 0 such that Φf ⊂ Cf 0 ∪ Φg and
        Φg ⊂ Cg0 ∪ Φf .
To each (oriented) edge e in E(Γl ) or E(Γs ) we associate the set Φe = Φe+ ∩ Φe− .
Note that Φe is always non-empty by construction.
Remark. If Φ is an irreducible classical root system, both Weyl graphs of Φ are
Cayley graphs of W = W (Φ), the Weyl group of Φ, but with respect to different
generating sets. The large Weyl graph Γl (Φ) is the Cayley graph with respect to
the set W \ {αlong (Φ)} where αlong (Φ) is the longest element of W relative to the
(standard) Coxeter generating set SΦ .

   The generating set corresponding to the small Weyl graph Γs (Φ) is harder to
describe. At this point we will just mention that it always contains the Coxeter
generating set SΦ , but it is equal to SΦ only for systems of type A2 .
Example 4.10. Figure 4.3 shows the Weyl graphs in the root systems of type A2
and B2 . The edges of the small Weyl graph are denoted by solid lines and ones in
the large Weyl graph are either by solid or by dotted lines.
       The structure of the large Weyl graph is very transparent.
Lemma 4.11. Let Φ be a root system.
   (a) The large Weyl graph Γl = Γl (Φ) is a regular graph with N vertices and
       degree N − 2, where N is the number of distinct Borel sets of Φ.
  (b) The eigenvalues of the adjacency matrix of Γl are N − 2 with multiplicity
       1, 0 with multiplicity N/2 and −2 with multiplicity N/2 − 1. Therefore the
       spectral gap of the Laplacian of Γl is equal to the degree of Γl .
Proof. (a) is clear. (b) A constant function is an eigenvector with eigenvalue N −
2, any “antisymmetric” function (one with F (x) = −F (x̄) where x and x̄ are
opposite vertices) has eigenvalue 0, and the space of antisymmetric functions has
dimension N/2. Finally, any “symmetric” function with sum 0 is an eigenfunction
with eigenvalue −2, and the space of such functions has dimension N/2 − 1.       

  The role played by the small Weyl graph in this paper will be discussed at the
end of this section. The key property we shall use is the following lemma:
Lemma 4.12. Let Φ be a regular root system. Then the graph Γs (Φ) is connected.
Proof. Let f, g ∈ F be two functionals such that Φf and Φg are distinct. We prove
that there exists a path in Γs from f to g by downward induction of |Φf ∩Φg |. If Φf
and Φg are co-maximal, then f and g are connected (by an edge) in the small Weyl
graph Γs by Lemma 4.4 and Corollary 4.9. If Φf and Φg are not co-maximal, then
by Lemma 4.4 there exists h such that Φf ∩ Φg is properly contained in Φh ∩ Φf
              PROPERTY (T ) FOR GROUPS GRADED BY ROOT SYSTEMS                     27


and Φh ∩ Φg . By induction, there are paths that connects h with both f and g.
Hence f and g are connected by a path in Γs .                               

Corollary 4.13. Both large and small Weyl graphs of any irreducible classical root
system of rank ≥ 2 are connected.
  We have computed the diameter of the small Weyl graph for some root systems,
and in all these examples the diameter is at most 3. We believe that this is true in
general.
Conjecture 4.14. If Φ is an irreducible classical root system of rank ≥ 2, then the
diameter of Γs is at most 3.
4.4. Groups graded by root systems.
Definition. Let Φ be a root system and G a group. A Φ-grading of G (or just
grading of G) is a collection of subgroups {Xα }α∈Φ of G, called root subgroups such
that
    (i) G is generated by ∪Xα ;
   (ii) For any α, β ∈ Φ, with α 6∈ R<0 β, we have
                   [Xα , Xβ ] ⊆ hXγ | γ = aα + bβ ∈ Φ, a, b ≥ 1i
If {Xα }α∈Φ is a collection of subgroups satisfying (ii) but not necessarily (i), we
will simply say that {Xα }α∈Φ is a Φ-grading (without specifying the group).
   Each grading of a group G by a root system Φ determines canonical graph of
groups decompositions of G over the large and small Weyl graphs of Φ. The vertex
and edge subgroups in these decompositions are defined as follows.
Definition. Let Φ be a root system, G a group and {Xα }α∈Φ a Φ-grading of G.
For each f ∈ V(Γl ) = V(Γs ) we set
                               Gf = hXα | α ∈ Φf i,
and for each e ∈ E(Γl ) ⊃ E(Γs ) we set
                               Ge = hXα | α ∈ Φe i.
We will call Gf the Borel subgroup of G corresponding to f .
Remark. We warn the reader that our use of the term ‘Borel subgroup’ is poten-
tially misleading. Assume that Φ is classical, irreducible and reduced. Let F be
a field and G = EΦ (F ) = GΦ (F ) the corresponding simply-connected Chevalley
group over F . Let {Xα }α∈Φ be the root subgroups (relative to the standard torus
H), so that {Xα } is a Φ-grading of G. Then Borel subgroups of G in our sense are
smaller than Borel subgroups in the sense of Lie theory. In fact, Borel subgroups in
our sense are precisely the unipotent radicals of those Borel subgroups in the sense
of Lie theory which contain H. Equivalently, our Borel subgroups are maximal
unipotent subgroups of G normalized by H.
Example 4.15. If G is a group graded by a root system of type A2 , Figure 4.4
shows the canonical decomposition of G over the large Weyl graph of A2 , called the
“magic graph” in [EJ].

Definition. Let {Xα } be a Φ-grading of a group G. For each f ∈ F(Φ), the core
subgroup GCf of Gf is the subgroup generated by the root subgroups in the core,
that is,
                             GCf = hXα | α ∈ Cf i.
Lemma 4.16. In the above notations, for each f ∈ F the core subgroup GCf is a
normal subgroup of Gf .
Proof. This is an immediate consequence of Lemma 4.6.                                                                                                             

Definition. Let Φ be a root system and {Xα }α∈Φ a Φ-grading.
      (i) Take any Borel subset Φf (with f ∈ F(Φ)) and any root γ ∈ Cf . We will
          say that the grading {Xα } is strong at the pair (γ, Φf ) if
                                                     Xγ ⊆ hXβ | β ∈ Φf and β 6∈ Rγi.
      (ii) We will say that the grading {Xα } is strong if {Xα } is strong at every pair
           (γ, Φf ) (with γ ∈ Cf ).
     (iii) Given an integer k, we will say that the grading {Xα }α∈Φ is k-strong if for
           any irreducible subsystem Ψ of rank k of Φ the grading {Xα }α∈Ψ is strong.


Remark. In § 7 and § 8 we will need to verify that the natural gradings of certain
Steinberg groups and twisted Steinberg groups are strong. With the exception of
§ 8.8, all examples we will consider satisfy the following property:
              PROPERTY (T ) FOR GROUPS GRADED BY ROOT SYSTEMS                        29


   For any two functionals f, f 0 ∈ F(Φ) there exists an automorphism w ∈ Aut(G)
which permutes the root subgroups {Xα }α∈Φ between themselves, and the induced
action of w on Φ sends the Borel subset Φf to the Borel subset Φf 0 .
   In the presence of this property, in order to prove that the grading {Xα } is strong
it suffices to check that it is strong at (γ, Φf ) where f ∈ F(Φ) is a fixed functional
and γ runs over Cf . In each of our examples, we shall use a functional f such that
Φf is the set of positive roots (with respect to a fixed system of simple roots). To
simplify the terminology, we shall say that the grading is strong at γ if it is strong
at (γ, f ) for the f that we fixed.
Example 4.17. Let Φ be a root system of type A2 and let {Xγ }. A sufficient
condition for the grading to be strong is that [Xα , Xβ ] = Xα+β for any pairs of
roots α and β such that α + β is also a root. This condition is also necessary under
the additional assumption that every element in a Borel subgroup can be expressed
uniquely as a product of elements in the 3 root subgroups (put in some fixed order).
Example 4.18. Let Φ be a root system of type B2 and let {Xγ } be a Φ-grading.
Assume that there exists an abelian group R such that each of the root subgroups
{Xγ } is isomorphic to R; thus we can denote the elements of Xγ by {xγ (r) : r ∈ R}
so that xγ (r + s) = xγ (r)xγ (s).
   Now let {α, β} be a base of Φ, with α a long root. Let f be any functional such
that ∂Φf = {α, β, α+β, α+2β} (in which case Cf = {α+β, α+2β}). By definition
of grading there exist functions p, q : R × R → R such that
             [xα (r), xβ (s)] = xα+β (p(r, s))xα+2β (q(r, s)) for all r, s ∈ S
Then the grading is strong at the pair (α + β, f ) (resp. (α + 2β, f )) whenever the
image of p (resp. q) generates R as a group.
   If Φ is a non-reduced root system, it is sometimes useful to slightly modify a
given Φ-grading using a simple operation called fattening:
Definition. Let Φ be a non-reduced root system and {Xα }α∈Φ a Φ-grading of
some group G. For each α ∈ Φ we set X      eα = hXaα : a ≥ 1i. We will say that
{Xeα }α∈Φ is the fattening of the grading {Xα }α∈Φ .

   It is easy to see that the fattening {X
                                         eα } is also Φ-grading. Moreover, {X
                                                                            eα } is
strong whenever {Xα } is strong.
4.5. A few words about the small Weyl graph. We end this section explaining
how the small Weyl graph and the notion of the core of a Borel set will be used in
this paper. Unlike the large Weyl graph, which plays a central role in the proof of
Theorem 5.1, the small Weyl graph is just a convenient technical tool.
   As discussed in § 3, given a group G graded by a regular root system Φ, Theo-
rem 5.1 for G will be proved by applying the generalized spectral criterion (Theo-
rem 3.3) to the canonical decomposition of G over the large Weyl graph Γl . The
small Weyl graph Γs will be used to verify hypothesis (iii) in Theorem 3.3.
   In fact, for many root systems we could use a different definition of the core of a
Borel subset (leading to a different small Weyl graph and different core subgroups)
which would still work for applications in § 5. We could not make the cores any
larger than we did (otherwise hypothesis (ii) in Theorem 3.3 would not hold), but
we could often make then smaller – the only properties we need is that the small
30       MIKHAIL ERSHOV, ANDREI JAIKIN-ZAPIRAIN, AND MARTIN KASSABOV


Weyl graph is connected (Lemma 4.12) and the core subgroups are normal in the
ambient vertex groups.
   For instance, if Φ is a simply-laced classical root system, we could let Cf consist of
just one root, namely, the root of maximal height in the Borel set Φf (this definition
coincides with ours for Φ = A2 ). In this case the small Weyl graph would become
the Cayley graph of the Weyl group W (Φ) with respect to the standard Coxeter
generating set. If Φ is a non-simply-laced classical root system, this seemingly more
natural definition of the core does not work, although we could still make the core
smaller except when Φ = B2 or BC2 .

           5. Property (T ) for groups graded by root systems
  In this section we prove Theorem 1.2, in fact a slightly generalized version of it
dealing with groups graded by regular (not necessarily classical) root systems.
Theorem 5.1. Let Φ be a regular root system, and let G be a group which admits
a strong Φ-grading {Xα }. Then ∪Xα is a Kazhdan subset of G, and moreover the
Kazhdan constant κ(G, ∪Xα ) is bounded below by a constant κΦ which depends only
on the root system Φ.
   Theorem 5.1 will be established by applying the generalized spectral criterion
from Theorem 3.3 to the canonical decomposition of G over the large Weyl graph of
Φ. Thus, we need to show that the hypotheses (i)-(iii) in Theorem 3.3 are satisfied
in this setting.
5.1. Estimates of codistances in nilpotent groups. We start by proving an
upper bound on codistances between certain families of subgroups in nilpotent
groups (Lemma 5.2 below). While this result is quite technical, once it is estab-
lished, verification of conditions (i) and (ii) in the proof of Theorem 5.1 will be
rather straightforward.

5.2. Estimates of codistances in Borel subgroups. For the rest of this section

f ∈ F representing that vertex. The vertex itself will also be denoted by f , and the
associated vertex subgroup will be denoted by Gf .

bound on codistances between edge subgroups of Gf :

Proof. By Claim 5.6 the group Gf,i contains the root subgroup Xβ for each β ∈ Φf

   This finishes the proof of Claim 5.7 and thus also the proof of Proposition 5.4. 


5.3. Norm estimates. In this subsection we establish the “norm inequality” (Corol-
lary 5.13) which is needed to verify hypothesis (iii) in Theorem 3.3. This inequality
will be proved by considering both the small and the large Weyl graphs. We note
that this is the only part of the paper where the small Weyl graph is used. In this
subsection we assume that V is a representation of the whole group G.
   Recall that the large Weyl graph Γl and the small Weyl graph Γs have the same
sets of vertices. Also recall that Ω0 (Γl , V ) = Ω0 (Γs , V ) is the set of all functions
from V(Γl ) = V(Γs ) to V and Ω0 (Γl , V ){Gν } the set of all functions g ∈ Ω0 (Γl , V )
such that g(f ) ∈ V Gf for each vertex f . Denote by dl and ds the difference operators
of Γl and Γs , respectively.
Lemma 5.8. Let g ∈ Ω0 (Γl , V ){Gν } = Ω0 (Γs , V ){Gν } . If Φ is a regular root system,
then
    (a) kds gk2 ≤ kdl gk2
    (b) kdl gk2 ≤ CΦ kds gk2 ,
where the constant CΦ depends only on the root system.
Proof. (a) is clear since Γs is a subgraph of Γl and (b) holds since Γs is connected.
Indeed, for each edge e in E(Γ

Proof. Let k = |Φe | and let {βj }1≤j≤k be the roots in Φe ordered so that

   By assumption, the vector v is Xβi+1 -invariant for each 0 ≤ i ≤ k − 1. Hence
πHi (v) is also Xβi+1 -invariant, so πHi (v) = πHi+1 (v). Combining these equalities
for all i we get πGe+ (v) = πHk (v) = πH0 (v) = πGRe (v).                         
  Now recall that Ω1 (Γl , V ) (resp. Ω1 (Γs , V )) is the space of all functions from

Example 5.12. Carefully doing the above estimates in the case of some “small” root

  Combining Lemma 5.8(b), Theorem 5.11 and the obvious inequality kρ1 (ds g)k ≤

Corollary 5.13. Let g be a function in Ω0 (Γl , V ){Gν } . Then
                      kdl gk2 ≤ AΦ kρ1 (dl g)k2 + BΦ kρ3 (dl g)k2 ,
where AΦ and BΦ are constants which depend on the root system Φ.
Example 5.14. In the case Φ = A2 , the above estimates show that one can take
AA2 = 5 and BA2 = 5. These bounds are not optimal — in [EJ] it is shown that
one can use AA2 = 3 and BA2 = 5.
5.4. Proof of Theorem 5.1.
Proof of Theorem 5.1. As explained at the beginning of this section, we shall apply
Theorem 3.3 to the canonical decomposition of G over the large Weyl graph Γ =
Γl Φ. Let us check that inequalities (i)-(iii) are satisfied.
   By Lemma 4.11 the spectral gap of the Laplacian λ1 (∆) is equal to the degree
of Γ. Hence in the notations of Theorem 3.3 we have p̄ = 1/2. Thus, (i) and (ii)
hold by Proposition 5.4. Finally, (iii) holds by Corollary 5.13.
   Since all parameters in these inequalities depend only on Φ, Theorem 3.3 yields
that κ(G, ∪Gf ) ≥ KΦ > 0, with KΦ depending only on Φ. Finally, to obtain the
same conclusion with Gf ’s replaced by root subgroups Xα ’s, we only need to observe
that each Gf lies in a bounded product of root subgroups, where the number of
factors does not exceed |Φ|/2.                                                    

                        6. Reductions of root systems
6.1. Reductions.
Definition. Let Φ be a root system in a space V = RΦ. A reduction of Φ is a
surjective linear map η : V → V 0 where V 0 is another nonzero real vector space.
The set Φ0 = η(Φ) \ {0} is called the induced root system. We will also say that η
is a reduction of Φ to Φ0 and symbolically write η : Φ → Φ0 .
Lemma 6.1. Let Φ be a root system, η a reduction of Φ, and Φ0 the induced root
system. Let {Xα }α∈Φ be a Φ-grading. For any α0 ∈ Φ0 put
                               Yα0 = hXα | η(α) = α0 i.
Then {Yα0 }α0 ∈Φ0 is a Φ0 -grading, which will be called the coarsened grading.
Proof. This is a direct consequence of the following fact: if A = hS1 i and B = hS2 i
are two subgroups of the same group, then [A, B] is contained in the subgroup
generated by all possible commutators in S1 ∪ S2 of length at least 2 with at least
one entry from S1 and S2 .                                                        
    A reduction η : Φ → Φ0 enables us to replace a grading of a given group G by
the “large” root system Φ by the coarsened grading by the “small” root system
Φ0 which may be easier to analyze. Note that the root subgroups of the coarsened
grading need not generate G since we “lose” root subgroups of the initial grading
which lie in ker η. Likewise, since different roots of Φ may map to the same root of
Φ0 , the coarsened grading need not be strong even if the initial grading is strong.
    Since we are mostly interested in strong gradings, we would like to have a natural
sufficient condition on η and the initial Φ-grading which ensures that the coarsened
Φ0 -grading is strong. If the only assumption on the initial Φ-grading {Xα } is that it
is strong, we would limit ourselves to reductions with trivial kernel (which are not
36        MIKHAIL ERSHOV, ANDREI JAIKIN-ZAPIRAIN, AND MARTIN KASSABOV


interesting). However, if we assume that {Xα } is k-strong for some k < rk(Φ), we
can let η be any k-good reduction, as defined below, which is much less restrictive.
Definition. Let k ≥ 2 be an integer. A reduction η of Φ to Φ0 is called k-good if
   (a) for any γ ∈ ker η ∩ Φ, there exists an irreducible regular subsystem Ψ of Φ
       of rank k such that γ ∈ Ψ and ker η ∩ Ψ ⊆ Rγ;
   (b) for any f ∈ F(Φ0 ), γ 0 ∈ Cf and γ ∈ Φ with η(γ) = γ 0 , there exists an
       irreducible subsystem Ψ of Φ of rank k and g ∈ F(Ψ) such that γ ∈ Cg ,
       η(Ψg ) ⊆ Φ0f and Ψ ∩ η −1 (Rγ 0 ) ⊆ Rγ.
Lemma 6.2. Let Φ be a root system, let η be a k-good reduction of Φ, and Φ0 =
η(Φ) \ {0} the induced root system. Let {Xα }α∈Φ be a k-strong grading of a group
G. Then the coarsened grading {Yα0 }α0 ∈Φ0 is a strong grading of G.
Proof. First let us show that G is generated by {Yα0 }. Since the subgroups {Xα }α∈Φ
generate G, it is enough to show that every Xγ lies in the subgroup generated
by {Yα0 }. This is clear if η(γ) 6= 0. Assume now that η(γ) = 0. Since the
reduction η is k-good, Φ has an irreducible regular subsystem Ψ of rank k such
that γ ∈ Ψ and ker η ∩ Ψ ⊆ Rγ. Since Ψ is regular, γ ∈ Cf for some f ∈
F(Ψ) by Corollary 4.9. Since the grading {Xα }α∈Φ is k-strong, Xγ lies in the
group generated by {Xα }α∈Ψf \Rγ ⊆ {Xα }α∈Φ\ker η , and so Xγ lies in the subgroup
generated by {Yα0 }.
   The fact that {Yα0 } is a strong grading of G follows directly from part (b) of the
definition of a k-good reduction and the assumption that the grading {Xα }α∈Φ is
k-strong.                                                                           
Corollary 6.3. Let Φ be a system such that any root lies in an irreducible subsystem
of rank k. If a Φ-grading of a group G is k-strong, then it is also strong.
Proof. The identity map RΦ → RΦ is clearly a reduction. It is k-good if and only
if any root of Φ lies in an irreducible subsystem of rank k.                  
6.2. Examples of good reductions. In this subsection we present several ex-
amples of good reductions that will be used in this paper. In particular, we will
establish the following result:
Proposition 6.4. Every irreducible classical root system of rank > 2 admits a
2-good reduction to an irreducible classical root system of rank 2.
     The following elementary fact can be proved by routine case-by-case verification.
Claim 6.5. Let Φ be a classical irreducible root system of rank ≥ 2. For α ∈ Φ
let NΦ (α) be the set of all β ∈ Φ such that span{α, β} ∩ Φ is an irreducible rank 2
system. Then for any α ∈ Φ, the set {α} ∪ NΦ (α) spans RΦ.
   Now let η : Φ → Φ0 be a reduction of root systems, where Φ is classical irreducible
of rank ≥ 2. Claim 6.5 implies that η always satisfies condition (a) in the definition
of a 2-good reduction. Indeed, ker η 6= RΦ (since Φ0 6= ∅), so given γ ∈ ker η ∩ Φ,
by Claim 6.5 there exists β ∈ NΦ (γ) \ ker η. Then clearly the subsystem Ψ =
(Rγ + Rβ) ∩ Φ has the required property.
   In order to speed up verification of condition (b) in the examples below we shall
use symmetries of the “large” root system Φ which project to symmetries of the
“small” root system Φ0 under η. Formally, we shall use the following observation:
              PROPERTY (T ) FOR GROUPS GRADED BY ROOT SYSTEMS                          37


  Suppose that a group Q acts linearly on RΦ preserving Φ and the subspace ker η.
Thus we have the induced action of Q on Φ0 given by
                         q η(α) = η(q α) for all α ∈ Φ, q ∈ Q.
Then to prove that η satisfies condition (b) in the definition of a k-good reduction
it suffices to check that condition for one representatives from each Q-orbit in
{(f, γ) : f ∈ F(Φ0 )/ ∼, η(γ) ∈ Cf0 }.
   In the following examples {e1 , . . . , en } is the standard orthonormal basis of Rn .
