                                η(x1 , · · · , xn+1 ) =         xi ai
                                                          i=1

is a reduction of An to In+1 . We shall prove that it is 2-good.
   First, we need to describe the boundary of Borel subsets of In . If f ∈ F(In ) then,
since f is different from 0 on In , we can find a permutation i1 , . . . , in of {1, . . . , n}
such that
(6.1)                        f (ai1 ) > f (ai2 ) > · · · > f (ain ),
so that the Borel set If is equal to {ais − ait : s < t}. We claim that the root
ais − ait lies in ∂If , the boundary of If , if and only if t = s + 1.
    The forward direction is clear since if t ≥ s + 2, then ais − ait = (ais − ais+1 ) +
(ais+1 − ait ), whence ais − ait ∈ Cf by Lemma 4.6.
    To prove the converse, first observe that {i2 , i3 } = {i1 − 1, i1 + 1} and for
1 ≤ k ≤ n/2 we have that i2k = i1 + k(i2 − i1 ) and i2k−1 = i1 + (k − 1)(i3 − i1 ). It
is then easy to see (algebraically or geometrically) that
(6.2)         ai2k−1 − ai2k ∈ R(a1 − a2 ) and ai2k − ai2k+1 ∈ R(a2 − a3 ).
Combined with what we already showed, this implies that ∂If ⊆ (R(a1 − a2 ) ∪
R(a2 − a3 )) ∩ If . On the other hand, it is clear that the boundary of any Borel
set in any root system of rank 2 is a union of two half-lines. Therefore, ∂If =
(R(a1 − a2 ) ∪ R(a2 − a3 )) ∩ If , and from (6.2) we deduce that ais − ais+1 ∈ ∂If for
all 1 ≤ s ≤ n − 1.
   Now let γ 0 ∈ Cf and take any γ ∈ An+1 with η(γ) = γ 0 . If γ = eit − eis , then
γ = ait − ais , and since γ 0 ∈ Cf , by the above argument t − s ≥ 2. Hence we can
 0

take Ψg to be {eit − eit+1 , eit+1 − eis , γ}. This proves that the reduction is 2-good.

                 7. Steinberg groups over commutative rings
   In this section we prove property (T ) for Steinberg groups of rank ≥ 2 over
finitely generated commutative rings and estimate asymptotic behavior of Kazhdan
constants.
              PROPERTY (T ) FOR GROUPS GRADED BY ROOT SYSTEMS                       41


7.1. Graded covers. Let Γ be a finite graph and G a group with a chosen de-
composition ({Gν }ν∈V(Γ) , {Ge }e∈E(Γ) ) over Γ. If H is another group with a de-
composition ({Hν , {He }}) over Γ, we will say that the decomposition ({Hν }, {He })
is isomorphic to ({Gν }, {Ge }) if there are isomorphisms ιν : Gν ∼    = Hν for each
ν ∈ V(Γ) and ιe : Ge ∼ = He for each e ∈ E(Γ) such that ιe+ |Ge = ιe and ιē = ιe .
   Among all groups which admit a decomposition over the graph Γ isomorphic
to ({Gν }, {Ge }) there is the “largest” one, which surjects onto any other group
with this property. This group will be called the cover of G corresponding to
({Gν }ν∈V(Γ) , {Ge }e∈E(Γ) ) and can be defined as the free product of the vertex sub-
groups {Gν }ν∈V(Γ) amalgamated along the edge subgroups {Ge }e∈E(Γ) . We will be
particularly interested in the special case of this construction dealing with decom-
positions associated to gradings by root systems.
Definition. Let G be a group, Φ a root system and {Xα }α∈Φ a Φ-grading of G.
Let Γl Φ be the large Weyl graph of Φ, and consider the canonical decomposition
of G over Γl Φ. The cover of G corresponding to this decomposition will be called
the graded cover of G with respect to the grading {Xα }.
   Graded covers may be also defined using generators and relations. Assume that
Gf = h∪α∈Φf Xα | Rf i for each f ∈ F(Φ). Then the graded cover of G with respect
to {Xα }α∈Φ is isomorphic to
                              h∪α∈Φ Xα | ∪f ∈F (Φ) Rf i.
Observe that if π : G → G0 is an epimorphism, and {Xα } is a Φ-grading of G,
then {π(Xα )} is a Φ-grading of G0 . If in addition π is injective on all the Borel
subgroups of G, then the graded covers of G and G0 coincide.
   Here is a simple observation about automorphisms of graded covers recorded
here for later use.
Definition. If G is a group and {Xα }α∈Φ is a grading of G, an automorphism
π ∈ Aut(G) will be called graded (with respect to {Xα }) if π permutes the root
subgroups {Xα } between themselves, and the induced action of π on Φ sends Borel
sets to Borel sets. The group of all graded automorphisms of G will be denoted by
Autgr (G).

Lemma 7.1. Let G be a group, {Xα } a grading of G and G  e the graded cover of
G with respect to {Xα }. Then each graded automorphism of G naturally lifts to
a graded automorphism of eG, and the obtained map Autgr (G) → Autgr ( e
                                                                      G) is a
monomorphism.
7.2. Steinberg groups over commutative rings. In this subsection we sketch
the definition of Steinberg groups and show that the natural grading of these groups
is strong. Our description of Steinberg groups follows Steinberg’s lecture notes on
Chevalley groups [St] and Carter’s book [Ca].
   We begin by recalling a few basic facts about simple complex Lie algebras (see
[Hu1] for more details). Let Φ be a reduced irreducible classical root system of
rank l and L a simple complex Lie algebra corresponding to Φ. Let H be a Car-
tan subalgebra of L. Then H is abelian, dim H = l, and we have the following
decomposition of L:
                                L = H ⊕ (⊕α∈Φ Lα ),
42        MIKHAIL ERSHOV, ANDREI JAIKIN-ZAPIRAIN, AND MARTIN KASSABOV


where Lα = {l ∈ L : [h, l]L = α(h)l for all h ∈ H} (as usual we consider Φ as a
subset of H∗ ). Moreover, each Lα is one dimensional.
   For any α, β ∈ Φ we put hβ, αi = 2(β,α)
                                         (α,α) , where (·, ·) is an admissible scalar
product on Φ (since (·, ·) is unique up to scalar multiples, the pairing h·, ·i is well
defined). For any α ∈ Φ let hα ∈ H be the unique element such that
(7.1)                                  β(hα ) = hβ, αi.
Note that if {α1 , . . . , αl } is a base of Φ, then hα1 , . . . , hαl is a basis of H.
Proposition 7.2. There exist nonzero elements xα ∈ Lα for α ∈ Φ such that
(7.2)             [xα , x−α ]L   = hα ,
                                   
                                      ±(r + 1)xα+β           if α + β ∈ Φ
(7.3)              [xα , xβ ]L   =                                         ,
                                           0                 if α + β 6∈ Φ
where r = max{s ∈ Z : β − sα ∈ Φ}.
   Any basis {hαi , xα : 1 ≤ i ≤ l, α ∈ Φ} of L with this property (for some choice of
Cartan subalgebra) is called a Chevalley basis. It is unique up to sign changes and
automorphisms of L. We will also need the following characterization of simple Lie
algebras in terms of Chevalley bases:
Lemma 7.3. Let the root system Φ and pairing h·, ·i be as above, and let {α1 , . . . , αl }
be a base of Φ. Let L be a finite-dimensional complex Lie algebra with basis
{xα }α∈Φ t {hi }li=1 such that
     (i) [xαi , x−αi ] = hi for 1 ≤ i ≤ l;
    (ii) [hi , xα ] = hα, αi ixα for 1 ≤ i ≤ l and α ∈ Φ;
   (iii) [hi , hj ] = 0 for 1 ≤ i, j ≤ l;
   (iv) elements {xα } satisfy (7.3).
Then L is a simple Lie algebra with root system Φ, that is, L ∼= L.
   From now on we fix a Chevalley basis B of L. Denote by LZ the subset of L
consisting of all linear combinations of the elements of B with integer coefficients.
By definition of a Chevalley basis, LZ is a Lie subring of L. For any commutative
ring R put LR = R ⊗Z LZ . The Lie bracket of LZ naturally extends to a Lie bracket
of LR .
Proposition 7.4. Let S = Q[t, s] and T = Z[t, s] be the polynomial rings in two
variables over Q and Z, respectively. Then for every α, β ∈ Φ, with α 6= β, we have
     (i) the derivation ad(txα ) = t ad(xα ) of LS is nilpotent;
                                      P∞ i
    (ii) xα (t) = exp(t ad(xα )) = i=0 ti! ad(xα )i is a well defined automorphism of
         LS which preserves LT ,
   (iii) there exist cij (α, β) ∈ Z such that the following equality holds:
                                           Y
(7.4)                   [xα (t), xβ (s)] =   xiα+jβ (cij (α, β)ti sj ),
                                            i,j

          where the product on the right is taken over all roots iα + jβ ∈ Φ, with
          i, j ∈ N, arranged in some fixed order. Moreover, the constants cij (α, β)
          depend only on the set {(i, j) ∈ Z × Z : iα + jβ ∈ Φ} and the chosen order.
     (iv) If α+β 6∈ Φ, then the product in (7.4) is empty (and thus [xα (t), xβ (s)] = 1).
          If α + β ∈ Φ, then c11 (α, β) = ±(r + 1) where r is given by (7.3). In
          particular, c11 (α, β) does not depend on the chosen order.
              PROPERTY (T ) FOR GROUPS GRADED BY ROOT SYSTEMS                         43


Remark. Up to sign, the constants cij (α, β) are independent of the choice of the
Chevalley basis (once the order in (7.4) has been fixed).

   Let Aα (t) be the matrix representing xα (t) with respect to B (note that LS is a
free S-module). By Proposition 7.4(ii), the entries of Aα (t) are in T .
   Now let R be a commutative ring R and let r ∈ R. Let xα (r) be the automor-
phism of LR represented by Aα (r) (the matrix obtained from Aα (t) by replacing t
by r) with respect to B. We denote by Xα = Xα (R) the set {xα (r) : r ∈ R}. This
is a subgroup of Aut(LR ) isomorphic to (R, +). By Proposition 7.4(iii), {Xα }α∈Φ
is a Φ-grading.
Definition. Let Φ be a reduced irreducible classical root system and R a commu-
tative ring.
    (a) The subgroup of Aut(LR ) generated by ∪α Xα is called the adjoint elemen-
         tary Chevalley group over R corresponding to Φ and will be denoted by
         Ead
           Φ (R).
    (b) The Steinberg group StΦ (R) is the graded cover of Ead
                                                             Φ (R) with respect to
         the grading {Xα }α∈Φ .
Remark. Elementary Chevalley groups of simply-connected type (and other non-
adjoint types) can be constructed in a similar way, except that the adjoint repre-
sentation of the Lie algebra LR should be replaced by a different representation.
The graded cover for each such group is isomorphic to StΦ (R).
   The Steinberg group StΦ (R) can also be defined as the group generated by the
elements {xα (r) : α ∈ Φ, r ∈ R} subject to the following relations for every
α 6= −β ∈ Φ and t, u ∈ R:
                 xα (t)xα (u)     =   xα (t + u)
                                            Y
               [xα (t), xβ (u)]   =                   xiα+jβ (cij (α, β)ti uj ),
                                      i,j∈N,iα+jβ∈Φ

where the constants cij (α, β) come from (7.4).
   Note that while the second definition of Steinberg groups has an advantage of
being explicit, the first one shows that the isomorphism class of StΦ (R) does not
depend on the choice of Chevalley basis.
Remark. Note that according to our definition the Steinberg group StA1 (R) is the
free product of two copies of (R, +). This definition does not coincide with the
usual definition in the literature, but it is convenient for the purposes of this paper.
Remark. If we do not assume that R is commutative, then LR does not have a
natural structure of a Lie algebra over R, and so the above construction of StΦ (R)
is not valid. However in the case Φ = An , we can still define the Steinberg group
as the graded cover of ELn+1 (R). When Φ 6= An we are not aware of any natural
way to define the Steinberg group StΦ (R) when R is noncommutative.
   In the special case Φ = A2 , the Steinberg group StA2 (R) can even be defined for
any alternative ring R (see [Fa, Appendix]).

   The following proposition will be used frequently in the rest of the paper. It
shows that some “natural” subgroups of Steinberg groups are quotients of Steinberg
groups.
44       MIKHAIL ERSHOV, ANDREI JAIKIN-ZAPIRAIN, AND MARTIN KASSABOV


Definition.
        P   Let Φ be a root system. A subset Ψ of Φ is called a weak subsystem
if Φ ∩ ( γ∈Ψ Zγ) = Ψ.
Proposition 7.5. Let Φ be a reduced irreducible classical root system and Ψ an
irreducible weak subsystem. Then Ψ is classical and the subgroup H of StΦ (R)
generated by {Xγ : γ ∈ Ψ} is a quotient of StΨ (R).
Proof. Note that Ψ is a root system. Moreover, Ψ is classical, since if (·, ·) is an
admissible scalar product on Φ, then (·, ·) restricted to Ψ is an admissible scalar
product on Ψ.
   Let L be a simple complex Lie algebra corresponding to Φ, choose a Cartan
subalgebra H of L, and define {hα }α∈Φ by (7.1). Let {hαi , xα : 1 ≤ i ≤ l, α ∈ Φ} a
Chevalley basis of L (relative to H). We claim that the Lie subalgebra LΨ generated
by {xα : α ∈ Ψ} is a simple complex Lie algebra corresponding to the root system
Ψ, and moreover, if {β1 , . . . , βm } is a base of Ψ, then {xα : α ∈ Ψ} t {hβi } is a
Chevalley basis of LΨ .
   By Lemma 7.3 (to be applied to Ψ) and definition of Chevalley basis, to prove
both statements it suffices to check that
     (i) the pairing h·, ·iΨ on Ψ is obtained from the pairing h·, ·iΦ on Φ by restric-
         tion;
    (ii) If α, β ∈ Ψ, then the value of r in relation (7.3) does not change if Φ is
         replaced by Ψ.
   Assertion (i) is clear since the scalar product on Ψ is obtained from the scalar
product on Φ by restriction. Assertion (ii) holds since Ψ is a weak subsystem and
the value of r in (7.3) depends only on the structure of the Z-lattice generated by
α and β.
   Thus, in view of Proposition 7.4(iii), the values of the coefficients cij (α, β), with
α, β ∈ Ψ, do not depend on whether we consider α, β as roots of Φ or Ψ. It follows
that the defining relations of StΨ (R) hold in H, so H is a quotient of StΨ (R). 
Remark. In most cases a weak subsystem of a classical root system is also a sub-
system, but not always. For instance, the long roots of G2 form a weak subsystem
of type A2 , but they do not form a subsystem. Note that the short roots of G2 do
not even form a weak subsystem.

   Next we explicitly describe some relations in the Steinberg groups corresponding
to root systems of rank 2.
Proposition 7.6. There exists a Chevalley basis such that
  (A) if Φ = A2 = {±α, ±β, ±(α + β), then
               [xα (t), xβ (t)] = xα+β (tu), [x−α (t), xα+β (u)] = xβ (tu).
     (B) if Φ = B2 = {±α, ±β, ±(α + β), ±(α + 2β), then
 [xα (t), xβ (u)] = xα+β (tu)xα+2β (tu2 ), [x−α (t), xα+β (u)] = xβ (tu)xα+2β (−tu2 ),
                         [xα+β (t), xβ (u)] = xα+2β (2tu).
     (G) if Φ = G2 = {±α, ±β, ±(α + β), ±(α + 2β), ±(α + 3β), ±(2α + 3β)}, then
            [xα (t), xβ (u)] = xα+β (tu)xα+2β (tu2 )xα+3β (tu3 )x2α+3β (t2 u3 ),
                            [xα (t), xα+3β (u)] = x2α+3β (tu),
              [xα+β (t), xβ (u)] = xα+2β (2tu)xα+3β (3tu2 )x2α+3β (3t2 u).
              PROPERTY (T ) FOR GROUPS GRADED BY ROOT SYSTEMS                       45


Proof. In each of those cases {α, β} is a base of Φ. The above relations which involve
only positive roots with respect to this base hold by [Hu2, Prop 33.3,33.4,33.5], for
a suitable choice of Chevalley basis. Moreover, the Chevalley basis for type B2
constructed in [Hu2, Prop 33.4] satisfies the additional conditions
       wα xα (t)wα−1 = x−α (−t),           wα xβ (t)wα−1 = xα+β (t),
       wα xα+β (t)wα−1 = xβ (−t),          wα xα+2β (t)wα−1 = xα+2β (t),
where wα = xα (1)x−α (−1)xα (1) is the Weyl group element corresponding to α.
Conjugating the relation [xα (t), xβ (u)] = xα+β (tu)xα+2β (tu2 ) by wα and using the
above conditions, we conclude that [x−α (t), xα+β (u)] = xβ (tu)xα+2β (−tu2 ). The
desired relation for type A2 involving −α can be obtained similarly.
   We warn the reader that notations in [Hu2] are different from ours, with the
roles of α and β switched for types B2 and G2 .                                    

Proposition 7.7. Let Φ be a reduced irreducible classical root system of rank l ≥ 2
and R a commutative ring, let G = StΦ (R) and {Xα : α ∈ Φ} the root subgroups
of G. Then {Xα : α ∈ Φ} is a k-strong grading of G for any 2 ≤ k ≤ l, and in
particular, it is strong.
Proof. Let Ψ be an irreducible subsystem of Φ of rank ≥ 2. Then by Proposition
7.5, Ψ is classical, and the subgroup H generated by {Xα : α ∈ Ψ} is a quotient of
the Steinberg group StΨ (R). Thus, the grading {Xα : α ∈ Ψ} of H is strong if the
natural Ψ-grading of StΨ (R) is strong. Since Ψ is regular, using Corollary 6.3 with
k = 2, we deduce that it is enough to prove Proposition 7.7 when l = k = 2. In this
case the result easily follows from Proposition 7.6. We illustrate this for Φ = G2 .
   Consider a functional f and let {α, β} be a base on which f takes positive values,
with α a long root. Then the core Cf is equal to {α + β, α + 2β, α + 3β, 2α + 3β}.
Since each of the maps (t, u) 7→ tu, (t, u) 7→ tu2 , (t, u) 7→ tu3 from R × R to R is
clearly surjective, the first two relations in Proposition 7.6(G) imply that
                    Xα+β      ⊆ [Xα , Xβ ]Xα+2β Xα+3β X2α+3β ,

                    Xα+2β     ⊆ [Xα , Xβ ]Xα+β Xα+3β X2α+3β ,

                    Xα+3β     ⊆ [Xα , Xβ ]Xα+β Xα+2β X2α+3β ,

                    X2α+3β    =     [Xα , Xα+3β ].
Hence the grading {Xα }α∈G2 is strong.                                              

7.3. Standard sets of generators of Steinberg groups. Let R be a commuta-
tive ring generated by T = {t0 = 1, t1 , . . . , td }. We denote by T ∗ the set
                        {ti1 · · · tik : 0 ≤ i1 < . . . < ik ≤ d} .
In the following proposition we describe a set of generators of StΦ (R) that we will
call standard.
Proposition 7.8. Let Φ be a reduced irreducible classical root system of rank at
least 2 and R a commutative ring generated by T = {t0 = 1, t1 , . . . , td }. Let Σ =
ΣΦ (T ) be the following set:
46         MIKHAIL ERSHOV, ANDREI JAIKIN-ZAPIRAIN, AND MARTIN KASSABOV


      (1) if Φ = An , Bn (n ≥ 3), Dn , E6 , E7 , E8 , F4 ,
                               Σ = {xα (t) : α ∈ Φ, t ∈ T },
      (2) if Φ = B2 , Cn ,
                                                                       
                           xα (t), t ∈ T        α ∈ Φ is a short root
                   Σ=                                                       ,
                           xα (t), t ∈ T ∗       α ∈ Φ is a long root
      (3) if Φ = G2 ,
                                                                       
                             xα (t), t ∈ T      α ∈ Φ is a long root
                    Σ=                                                      .
                             xα (t), t ∈ T ∗    α ∈ Φ is a short root
Then Σ generates StΦ (R).
Proof. First we consider the case Φ = An , Bn (n ≥ 3), Dn , E6 , E7 , E8 , F4 . We
prove by induction on k that for any γ ∈ Φ and any monomial m in variables from
T of degree k, the element xγ (m) lies in hΣi, the subgroup generated by Σ. This
statement clearly implies the proposition.
   The base of induction is clear. Assume that the statement is true for monomials
of degree ≤ k. Let m be a monomial of degree k + 1.
   If Φ = An , Dn , E6 , E7 , E8 , F4 , then we can find a subsystem Ψ of Φ isomorphic
to A2 which contains γ. We write γ = γ1 + γ2 , where γ1 , γ2 ∈ Ψ, and m = m1 m2 ,
where m1 , m2 are monomials of degree ≤ k. Then xγ (m) = [xγ1 (m1 ), xγ2 (m2 )]±1
and we can apply the inductive hypothesis.
   If Φ = Bn , n ≥ 3, then any long root lies in an irreducible subsystem isomorphic
to A2 , whence the statement holds when γ is a long root. Assume γ is a short root.
Then there are a long root α and a short root β such that γ = α + β. Note that α
and β generate a subsystem of type B2 . Without loss of generality we may assume
that the relations of Proposition 7.6 hold. Then we obtain that
                     xγ (m) = xα+β (m) = [xα (m), xβ (1)]xα+2β (−m).
Since the roots α and α + 2β are long, by induction xγ (m) lies hΣi.
   In the case Φ = B2 the proposition is an easy consequence of the following lemma
(we do not need the second part of this lemma now; it will be used later).
Lemma 7.9. Let {α, β} be a base of B2 with α a long root. Consider the semidirect
product StA1 (R) n N , where N = hXβ , Xα+β , Xα+2β i ⊂ StB2 (R) and the action of
StA1 (R) on N comes from the conjugation action of hXα , X−α i ⊂ StB2 (R) on N .
Let
     S1 = {xα (t), x−α (t) : t ∈ T ∗ ∪ T 2 };    S2 = {xβ (t), xα+β (t) : t ∈ T } and
                               ∗
     S3 = {xα+2β (t) : t ∈ T }.

the following hold:
    (1) G contains N ;
    (2) Xα+2β /([N, G] ∩ Xα+2β ) is of exponent 2 and generated by S3 .
Proof. Without loss of generality we may assume that the relations from Proposi-
tion 7.6 hold in StB2 (R).
   We prove by induction on k that for any γ ∈ {α+β, α+2β, β} and any monomial
m in T of degree k, the element xγ (m) lies in hSi. This clearly implies the first
statement.
              PROPERTY (T ) FOR GROUPS GRADED BY ROOT SYSTEMS                        47


   The base of induction is clear. Assume that the statement holds for all monomials

                 xα+2β (m1 m22 ) = [xα (m1 ), xβ (m2 )]xα+β (−m1 m2 ).

                       xα+β (m) = [xα (m), xβ (1)]xα+2β (−m)

(7.5)     xα+β (m) = [xα (t2 ), xβ (m1 )][xα (1), xβ (−tm1 )]xα+β (tm1 ) ∈ G.
   Case 3: γ = β. This case is analogous to Case 2, but this time we use the

  The proof of the second part is an easy exercise based on Proposition 7.6 and

   We now go back to the proof of Proposition 7.8. In the case Φ = B2 the result
follows from Lemma 7.9(1) since for any t ∈ T and long root γ ∈ B2 , the element
xγ (t2 ) can be expressed as a product of elements from Σ. For instance, in the case
γ = α + 2β we have
(7.6)                   xα+2β (t2 ) = [xα (1), xβ (t)]xα+β (−t).
   If Φ = Cn , then any root lies in a subsystem of type B2 , and so the result follows
from the previous case.
   Finally, consider the case Φ = G2 . The long roots of G2 form a weak subsystem
of type A2 , so Proposition 7.8 for type A2 (which we already established) and
Proposition 7.5 imply that long root subgroups of StG2 (R) lie in hΣi. It remains
to show that xγ (m) ∈ hΣi for any short root γ and any monic monomial m in T ,
which we will do by induction on the degree of m. By symmetry, it suffices to prove
that xα+2β (m) ∈ hΣi.
   If m is square-free, then m ∈ T ∗ , so xγ (m) ∈ Σ by definition; otherwise m = tu2
for some monomials t and u, with u 6= 1. By Proposition 7.6(C) we have
        xα+2β (tu2 ) = x2α+3β (−tu3 )xα+3β (−t2 u3 )[xα+β (u), x−α (t)]xβ (tu).
All factors on the right-hand side lie in hΣi, namely xα+β (u), xβ (tu) ∈ hΣi by the
induction hypothesis and x−α (t), x2α+3β (−tu3 ), xα+3β (−t2 u3 ) ∈ hΣi since the roots
−α, α + 3β and 2α + 3β are long. Thus, xα+2β (m) ∈ hΣi, as desired.                  
7.4. Property (T ) for Steinberg groups. In this subsection we establish prop-
erty (T ) for Steinberg groups (of rank ≥ 2) over finitely generated rings and obtain
asymptotic lower bounds for the Kazhdan constants. It will be convenient to use
the following notation.
  If κ is some quantity depending on N-valued parameters n1 , . . . , nr (and possibly
some other parameters) and f : Nr → R>0 is a function, we will write
                                  κ < f (n1 , . . . , nr )
if there exists an absolute constant C > 0 such that κ ≥ Cf (n1 , . . . , nr ) for all
n1 , . . . , nr ∈ N.
48        MIKHAIL ERSHOV, ANDREI JAIKIN-ZAPIRAIN, AND MARTIN KASSABOV


    Let Φ be a reduced irreducible classical root system of rank ≥ 2 and R a finitely
generated ring (which is commutative if Φ is not of type A). By Proposition 7.7 the
standard grading {Xα } of StΦ (R) is strong, so to prove property (T ) it suffices to
check relative property (T ) for each of the pairs (StΦ (R), Xα ). However, in order
to obtain a good bound for the Kazhdan constant of StΦ (R) with respect to a finite
generating set of the form ΣΦ (T ) (as defined in Proposition 7.8), we need to proceed
slightly differently.
    We shall use a good reduction of Φ to a root system of rank 2 (of type A2 , B2 ,
BC2 or G2 ) described in § 6.2. Proposition 7.7 implies that the coarsened grading
{Yβ } of StΦ (R) is also strong, so Theorem 5.1 can be applied to this grading, this
time yielding a much better bound for the Kazhdan constant.
    To complete the proof of property (T ) for StΦ (R) we still need to establish
relative property (T ), this time for the pairs (StΦ (R), Yβ ). Qualitatively, this is not
any harder than proving relative (T ) for (StΦ (R), Xα ); however, we also need to
explicitly estimate the corresponding Kazhdan ratios (which will affect the eventual
bound for the Kazhdan constant of StΦ (R) with respect to a finite generating set).
Terminology: For brevity, in the sequel instead of saying relative property (T ) for
the pair (G, H) we will often say relative property (T ) for H if G is clear from the
context.
   Our main tool for proving relative property (T ) is the following result of Kass-
abov [Ka1] which generalizes Theorem 2.3 and will be proved in Appendix A in a
slightly extended form (see Theorem A.8).
Theorem 7.10 (Kassabov). Let n ≥ 2 and R a ring generated by T = {1 =
t0 , t1 , . . . , td }. Let {α1 , · · · , αn } be a system of simple roots of An . Consider the
semidirect product G = StAn−1 (R) n N , where N ∼                 = Rn is the subgroup of StAn (R)

from the action of the subgroup hXγ : γ ∈ Rα2 + . . . + Rαn i of StAn (R) on N
(we will refer to this action as the standard action of StAn−1 (R) on Rn ). Let
S = ΣAn−1 (T ) ∪ (ΣAn (T ) ∩ N ) and let G0 = hS, N i. Then

   Before turning to the case-by-case verification of relative property (T ) (which is
the main part of the proof of Theorem 7.12 below), we briefly summarize how this
will be done for different root systems. Let Φ be a reduced irreducible classical root
system with rk(Φ) ≥ 2.
    (1) If Φ is simply-laced, relative property (T ) for the root subgroups of StΦ (R)
        will follow almost immediately from Theorem 7.10 (with the aid of Propo-
        sition 7.5).
    (2) If Φ is non-simply-laced and Φ 6= B2 , relative property (T ) for some of
        the root subgroups (either short ones or long ones) will again follow from
        Theorem 7.10. To prove relative property (T ) for the remaining root sub-
        groups, we will show that each of them is contained in a bounded product
        of a finite set and root subgroups for which relative (T ) has already been
        established.
    (3) Finally, the most difficult case Φ = B2 has almost been established in
        Corollary 7.11.
Explicit bounds for the Kazhdan constants and Kazhdan ratios will follow from
Observation 2.2 and Lemma 2.4; these results will be often used without further
mention.
  We are now ready to prove the main result of this section.

                          8. Twisted Steinberg groups
8.1. Constructing twisted groups. In this subsection we introduce a general
method for constructing new groups graded by root systems from old ones using the
machinery of twists. The method generalizes the construction of twisted Chevalley
groups [St].
   Let Φ be a root system, G a group and {Xα }α∈Φ a Φ-grading of G. Let Q ⊂
Aut(G) be a group of automorphisms of G such that
     (i) Each element of Q is a graded automorphism (as defined in § 7.1), so that
         there is an induced action of Q on Φ.
    (ii) Q acts linearly on Φ, that is, the action of Q on Φ extends to an R-linear
         action of Q on the real vector space spanned by Φ.
Remark. In all our applications Q will be a finite group (in fact, usually a cyclic
group).
  Let V = RΦ be the R-vector space spanned by Φ. Suppose we are given another
R-vector space W and a reduction η : V → W such that
(8.1)                  η(qα) = η(α) for any α ∈ Φ and q ∈ Q.
Let Ψ = η(Φ) \ {0} be the induced root system and let {Yβ }β∈Ψ be the coarsened
grading, that is, Yβ = hXα : η(α) = βi. Finally, let Zβ = YβQ be the set of Q-fixed
points in Yβ , and assume that the following additional condition holds:
   (iii) {Zα }α∈Ψ is a Ψ-grading.
              PROPERTY (T ) FOR GROUPS GRADED BY ROOT SYSTEMS                          53


Then we define the twisted group G       d Q to be the graded cover of hZ : α ∈ Ψi
                                                                           α
with respect to the Ψ-grading {Zα }α∈Ψ .
    Here is a slightly technical but easy-to-use criterion which ensures that condition
(iii) holds. This criterion will be applicable in all of our examples.

Proposition 8.1. Let Ψ0 be the set of all roots in Ψ which are not representable
as aγ for some other γ ∈ Ψ and a > 1 (in particular, Ψ0 = Ψ if Ψ is reduced).
Assume that
    (a) For any γ, δ ∈ Ψ such that δ = aγ with a ≥ 1, we have Yδ ⊆ Yγ .
    (b) For any Borel subset B of Ψ, any element of hYγ iγ∈B can be uniquely
                   Qk
        written as i=1 yγi where γ1 , . . . , γk are the roots in B ∩ Ψ0 taken in some
        fixed order and yγi ∈ Yγi for all i.
Then {Zα }α∈Ψ is a Ψ-grading.

Proof. Let α, β ∈ Ψ such that β 6∈ R<0 α, in which case there exists a Borel subset
B containing both α and β. Let Ω = {γ ∈ Ψ : γ = aα + bβ with α, β ≥ 1}, and
let γ1 , . . . , γk be the roots in B ∩ Ψ0 (as in condition (b)). Let I be the set of all
i ∈ {1, . . . , k} such that R≥1 γi ∩ Ω 6= ∅. For each i ∈ I let δi = ai γi such that
δi ∈ Ω and ai ∈ R≥1 is smallest possible.
   Now take any z ∈ Zα and w ∈ Zβ . Since    Q     {Yγ }γ∈Ψ is a Ψ-grading, using condi-
tion (a) it is easy to show that [z, w] = i∈I yδi whereQyδi ∈ Yδi for each i. Since z
and w are fixed by Q, for any q ∈ Q we have [z, w] = i∈I q(yδi ).
   Thus, we have obtained two factorizations for [z, w], and since Yδi ⊆ Yγi , they
both satisfy the requirement in (b). Therefore, (b) implies that q(yδi ) = yδi for
each i. Hence yδi ∈ YδQi = Zδi for each i ∈ I, and so [z, w] ∈ hZγ : γ ∈ Ωi.           

   If Q is finite, one always has a natural choice for the pair (W, η) satisfying (8.1).
Indeed, by condition (ii) the action of Q on Φ extends to a linear action of Q on V .
Then we can take W = V Q , the subspace of Q-invariant vectors and η : V → W
the natural projection, that is,
                                            1 X

In fact, in all our examples the pair (W, η) will be of this form up to isomorphism,
but it will be more convenient to define W and η first and then check (8.1) rather
than realize W as the subspace of Q-invariant vectors in V .

Remark. If Q and Q0 are conjugate in the group Autgr (G) of graded automor-
phisms of G, then the corresponding twisted groups G

   We will discuss in detail six families of twisted groups. The first five families will
all be of the following form, while the construction of the sixth family will involve
minor modifications. Let Φ be classical, reduced and irreducible of rank ≥ 2. We
take G = StΦ (R) for some ring R, which is commutative if Φ is not of type A. The
acting group Q will be a finite (usually cyclic) subgroup of Aut(G) whose elements
are compositions of diagram, ring and diagonal automorphisms (as defined in § 8.2).

   We note that the term ‘twisted Chevalley group’ usually has a more restricted
meaning – instead of all possible finite groups of automorphisms Q as above, one
only considers those which are used in (canonical) realizations of finite simple groups
of twisted Lie type. In this section we will mostly deal with the Steinberg covers
for these types of twisted Chevalley groups. The obtained Steinberg groups are
summarized below and will be studied in Examples 1-5.
      1. Groups Stω  Cn (R, ∗) where R is a ring, ∗ is an involution on R and ω is a
         central unit of R satisfying ω ∗ = ω −1 . These groups are Steinberg covers
         for hyperbolic unitary groups (see [HO]). The special case ω = 1 corre-
         sponds to twisted Chevalley groups of type 2 A2n−1 (unitary groups in even
         dimension).
      2. Groups StBCn (R, ∗) where R is a ring and ∗ is an involution on R. These
         groups are Steinberg covers for twisted Chevalley groups of type 2 A2n (uni-
         tary groups in odd dimension).
      3. Groups StBn (R, σ) where R is a commutative ring and σ is an involution
         on R. These groups are Steinberg covers for twisted Chevalley groups of
         type 2 Dn .
      4. Groups StG2 (R, σ) where R is a commutative ring and σ an automorphism
         of R of order 3. These groups are Steinberg covers for twisted Chevalley
         groups of type 3 D4 .
      5. Groups StF4 (R, σ) where R is a commutative ring and σ an involution of
         R. These groups are Steinberg covers for twisted Chevalley groups of type
         2
           E6 .
Remark. Our notations for the twisted Steinberg groups are chosen in such a way
that the subscript indicates the root system by which this twisted Steinberg group
is naturally graded.
   In Example 3 we shall define a more general family of twisted groups (which
will include the groups StBn (R, σ) as a special case) using an observation that the
(classical) Steinberg group StDn (R) arises as the twisted Steinberg group St−1Cn (R, ∗)
(from Example 1) in the special case when ∗ is trivial (and R is commutative).
   In the last example (Example 6) we construct certain groups St2 F4 (R, ∗), where
R is a commutative ring of characteristic 2 and ∗ : R → R is an injective homo-
morphism such that (r∗ )∗ = r2 . These groups are graded by a root system in R2
with 24 roots and can be defined as graded covers of certain “algebraic-like” groups
constructed by Tits [Ti]. We note that the standard definition of twisted Cheval-
ley groups of type 2 F4 is only valid when R is a perfect field, in which case they
coincide with Tits’ groups. Unlike Examples 1-5, in the construction of the groups
St2 F4 (R, ∗), the initial group G will not be the entire Steinberg group StF4 (R), but
              PROPERTY (T ) FOR GROUPS GRADED BY ROOT SYSTEMS                      55


certain subgroup of it. The general twisting procedure will also be slightly modified
in this example, as we will have to apply the fattening operation (defined in § 4.4)
to the coarsened grading {Yβ }.
8.2. Graded automorphisms of Ead      Φ (R) and StΦ (R). In this section we describe
some natural families of graded automorphisms of (non-twisted) adjoint elementary
Chevalley groups and Steinberg groups. Each automorphism will be defined via its
action on the root subgroups, and we will need to justify that it can be extended
to the entire group.
   In the case of elementary Chevalley groups and Steinberg groups over commu-
tative rings the following observation will provide the justification:
Observation 8.2. Let Φ be a reduced irreducible classical root system, R a commu-
tative ring and LR the R-Lie algebra of type Φ, as defined in § 7. Let f ∈ Aut(LR )
be an automorphism which permutes the root subspaces of LR . Then Ead     Φ (R), con-
sidered as a subgroup of Aut(LR ), is normalized by f , and moreover, the conjugation
by f permutes the root subgroups {Xα (R)} of Ead  Φ (R). Thus f naturally induces a
graded automorphism of Ead Φ (R)  and  hence also induces a graded automorphism of
StΦ (R) by Lemma 7.1.
   If G = StAm (R), with R noncommutative, the existence of the automorphism of
G with a given action on the root subgroups is easy to establish using the standard
presentation of StAm (R) recalled below.
   As usual, we realize Am as the subset {ei − ej : 1 ≤ i 6= j ≤ m + 1} of Rm+1 .
The group StAm (R) has generators {xei −ej (r) : 1 ≤ i 6= j ≤ m + 1, r ∈ R} and
relations
                     xei −ej (r + s) = xei −ej (r)xei −ej (s) and
                                        
                                         xei −el (rs) if j = k and i 6= l
           [xei −ej (r), xek −el (s)] =    xe −e (−sr) if j 6= k and i = l
                                         k j
                                                 0           if j 6= k and i 6= l
   In each of the following examples we fix a ring R and a reduced irreducible
classical root system Φ, and G will denote one of the groups Ead
                                                              Φ (R) or StΦ (R),
unless additional restrictions are imposed.
   Type I: ring automorphisms. Let σ be an automorphism of the ring R. Then
we can define the automorphism ϕσ of G by
                       ϕσ (xα (r)) = xα (σ(r)), α ∈ Φ, r ∈ R.
If R is commutative, ϕσ is well defined since it is induced (as in Observation 8.2)
by the automorphism of LR which sends r ⊗ l (where l ∈ LZ and r ∈ R) to σ(r) ⊗ l.
   If R is arbitrary and G = StAn (R), the automorphism ϕσ is well defined since it
clearly respects the defining relations of G.
   Type II: diagonal automorphisms. Let Z(R)× be the group of invertible el-
ements of Z(R), let ZΦ denote the Z-span of Φ, and let µ : ZΦ → Z(R)× be a
homomorphism. Then we can define the automorphism χµ of G given by
                      χµ (xα (r)) = xα (µ(α)r), α ∈ Φ, r ∈ R.
If R is commutative, χµ is well defined since it is induced by the automorphism of
LR which fixes hα and sends xα to µ(α)xα for any α ∈ Φ.
   As for type I, if R is arbitrary and G = StAn (R), the automorphism χµ is well
defined since it respects the defining relations.
56      MIKHAIL ERSHOV, ANDREI JAIKIN-ZAPIRAIN, AND MARTIN KASSABOV


   Type III: root system automorphisms (commutative case). In this example
we assume that R is commutative. Let V = RΦ be the R-span of Φ, and let π
be an automorphism of V which stabilizes Φ (equivalently, we can start with an
automorphism of Φ and uniquely extend it to an automorphism of V ). Then there
are constants γα = ±1(α ∈ Φ) such that the map
                      λπ (xα (r)) = xπ(α) (γα r), α ∈ Φ, r ∈ R,
can be extended to an automorphism of G.
   The existence of an automorphism of LR which induces λπ is a consequence of
the Isomorphism Theorem for simple Lie algebras ([Ca, Theorem 3.5.2], see also
[Ca, Proposition 12.2.3]).
   Note that there is no canonical choice for the constants γα (except when Φ = An ),
so in our notation λπ is only unique up to a diagonal automorphism (which acts as
multiplication by ±1 on each root subgroup).
   Type III’: mixed automorphisms of StAm (R). In this example we assume that
G = StAm (R) and R is arbitrary. Let V be the R-span of Am . It is well known
that every automorphism of Am has the form
                      a(π, δ) : ei − ej 7→ (−1)δ (eπ(i) − eπ(j) )
for some permutation π ∈ Σm+1 and δ = 0, 1. In particular, Aut(Am ) has order
2(m + 1)! (if m ≥ 2), and it is easy to see that the automorphisms with δ = 0 are
precisely the elements of the Weyl group of Am .
   If R is commutative, we have already associated an automorphism of G of type III
to each element of Aut(Am ). The type III automorphism of G corresponding to
a(π, 0) ∈ Aut(Am ) can be defined even if R is not commutative. It will be denoted
by λ+π and is given by

                  λ+
                   π (xei −ej (r)) = xeπ(i) −eπ(j) (r), α ∈ Φ, r ∈ R.

   Similarly, if R is commutative, we will denote by λ−
                                                      π the type III automorphism
of G corresponding to a(π, 1) ∈ Aut(Am ). It is given by
                 λ−
                  π (xei −ej (r)) = xeπ(j) −eπ(i) (−r), α ∈ Φ, r ∈ R.

The formula for λ−
                 π will not define an automorphism of G if R is noncommutative.
However, if we are given an anti-automorphism ∗ of R, for each π ∈ Σm+1 we can
define an automorphism λ− π,∗ of G by setting

                λ−                                    ∗
                 π,∗ (xei −ej (r)) = xeπ(j) −eπ(i) (−r ), α ∈ Φ, r ∈ R.

These automorphisms will be called mixed.
   Note that if R is commutative, then λ−                               −
                                        π,∗ is just the composition of λπ and the
ring automorphism ϕ∗ .
   The collection of twisted groups that can be constructed using these four types of
automorphisms and their compositions is clearly too large for case-by-case analysis
and is beyond the scope of this paper. We shall concentrate on automorphisms
which yield natural analogues of twisted Chevalley groups listed at the end of § 8.1.
   Among all root system automorphisms of particular importance are diagram
automorphisms – the ones induced by an automorphism of the Dynkin diagram
of Φ. For instance, in the case Φ = Am , there is unique (non-trivial) diagram
automorphism (for a given choice of simple roots) – in the above notations it is the
automorphism λ−  π where π ∈ Σm+1 is given by π(i) = m+2−i. Each of the twisted
             PROPERTY (T ) FOR GROUPS GRADED BY ROOT SYSTEMS                     57


Chevalley groups of type k Φ, where Φ = An , Dn or E6 and k = 2, or Φ = D4 and
k = 3, is obtained from Ead
                         Φ (R) using the twisting by the composition of a diagram
automorphism and a ring automorphism of the same order k.

8.3. Unitary Steinberg groups over non-commutative rings with involu-
tion. In this subsection we shall define (twisted) Steinberg groups corresponding
to (quasi-split) unitary groups and, in the case of even dimension, their general-
izations, called hyperbolic unitary Steinberg groups. We shall establish property
(T ) for most of those groups. To simplify the exposition, we will not provide ex-
plicit estimates for the Kazhdan constants, although in most cases reasonably good
estimates can be obtained by adapting the arguments from § 7.
   Throughout this subsection we fix a ring R, and let ∗ : R → R be an involution,
that is, an anti-automorphism of order ≥ 2.
   As we already stated, in the classical setting unitary groups are obtained from
Chevalley groups of type Am via twisting by the order 2 automorphism
             Dyn∗ = λ−
                     π,∗ where π is the permutation i 7→ m + 2 − i.

In even dimension (that is, if m is odd), there is an interesting generalization of
this construction, where instead of Dyn∗ one uses the composition of Dyn∗ with a
suitable diagonal automorphism of order 2.
   To each ω ∈ Z(R)× we can associate a homomorphism Tω : ZAm → Z(R)×
given by
                          
                           1     if i, j ≤ (m + 1)/2 or i, j > (m + 1)/2
          Tω (ei − ej ) =    ω    if i ≤ (m + 1)/2 < j
                           −1
                            ω     if j ≤ (m + 1)/2 < i
Note that the homomorphism T1 : ZAm → Z(R)× is the trivial homomorphism.
  For each such ω we define the automorphism qω of StAm (R) given by
(8.3)                            qω = Dyn∗ χTω .
(recall that χTω is a diagonal automorphism, defined in § 8.2).
   Now let
         U (R) = {r ∈ R× : rr∗ = 1}     and    U (Z(R)) = U (R) ∩ Z(R).
It is easy to see that if m is odd and ω ∈ U (Z(R)), then qω has order 2.
   The groups obtained from Chevalley groups of type Am via twisting by qω (with
m odd and ω ∈ U (Z(R))) are called hyperbolic unitary groups. These groups have
been originally defined by Bak [Bak1] and are discussed in detail in the book by
Hahn and O’Meara [HO] (see also [Bak2]).
Remark. It is easy to show that if χ is any diagonal automorphism of the Cheval-
ley group SLm+1 (R) such that the composition Dyn∗ χ has order 2, then χ is
graded conjugate (in fact, conjugate by a diagonal automorphism) to qω for some
ω ∈ U (Z(R)) if m is odd, and graded conjugate to Dyn∗ if m is even; see also
Observation 8.4 below. This yields a simple characterization of hyperbolic unitary
groups among all twisted Chevalley groups.

  Before turning to Example 1, we introduce some additional terminology from
[HO] (we note that our notations are different from [HO]).

       because the long root subgroups in the Cn -grading on St1Cn (R, id, {0}) are
       trivial, and we can “remove” those roots to obtain a Dn -grading.

  We now turn to the proof of property (T ) for hyperbolic unitary Steinberg groups.
Lemma 8.5. Let R be a ring with involution ∗, let ω ∈ U (Z(R)), and let J be a
form parameter of (R, ∗, ω).
   (a) If n ≥ 3, the Cn -grading on Stω Cn (R, ∗, J) is strong.
   (b) Assume that the left ideal of R generated by J equals R. Then the Cn -
       grading on StωCn (R, ∗, J) is 2-strong (in particular, the grading is strong if
       n = 2).
Proof. (a) By definition, we need to check that the grading is strong at (γ, B) for
every Borel subset B and γ ∈ C(B), the core of B, and by symmetry it suffices to
consider the case when B is the standard Borel. If γ ∈ C(B) is a long root, the
grading is strong at (γ, B) by relations (E3) with s = 1. If n ≥ 3 and γ ∈ C(B) is
a short root, the grading is strong at (γ, B) by relations (E1) or (E4).
   (b) If n = 2, the grading is strong at short root subgroups by relations (E3).
The same argument shows that the grading is 2-strong for any n ≥ 2.              

Proposition 8.6. Let R be a finitely generated ring with involution ∗, ω ∈ U (Z(R))
and J a form parameter of (R, ∗, ω). Assume that J is finitely generated as a form
parameter. The following hold:
   (a) The group H = Stω   Cn (R, ∗, J) has property (T ) for any n ≥ 3.
   (b) Assume in addition that ω = −1, 1R ∈ J (so, in particular, the left ideal of
       R generated by J equals R), and R is a finitely generated right module over
       its subring generated by a finite set of elements from J. Then the group
       St−1
          C2 (R, ∗, J) has property (T ).

Proof. Lemma 8.5 ensures that the Cn -grading is strong, so we only need to check
relative property (T ) for root subgroups.
   (a) Relations (E1) ensure that any short root subgroup Zγ can be put inside
a group which is a quotient of StA2 (R) = St3 (R) and hence the pair (H, Zγ ) has
relative property (T ). To prove relative (T ) for long root subgroups we realize each
of them as a subset of a bounded product of short root subgroups and some finite
set. Without loss of generality, we will establish the required factorization for the
long root γ = 2ε1 .
   Let T be a finite set which generates J as a form parameter of (R, ∗, ω). By the
remark following the definition of a form parameter, any r ∈ J can be written as

r = t∈T st ts∗t + (u − ωu∗ ) for some st , u ∈ R. Relations (E2) and (E3) yield the

Observation 8.7. The group StBCn (R, ∗, {0}) is isomorphic to St1Cn (R, ∗).
Proposition 8.8. Let R be a finitely generated ring with involution ∗. Assume
that {r ∈ I : ∃t ∈ R, rr∗ = t + t∗ } is finitely generated as a left ideal and Asym(R)
is finitely generated as a form parameter of (R, ∗, 1). The following hold:
     (a) The group StBCn (R, ∗, I) has property (T ) for any n ≥ 3.
     (b) Assume in addition that there exists an invertible antisymmetric element
          µ ∈ Z(R), and R is a finitely generated module over a ring generated by a
          finite set of symmetric elements. Then the group StBC2 (R, ∗, I) has property
          (T ).
Proof. We shall prove (a) and (b) simultaneously. The fact that the grading is
strong in both cases is verified as in Lemma 8.5, so we only need to check relative
property (T ). Observe that the set Ψ of long and double roots in BCn is a weak
subsystem of type Cn , so the corresponding root subgroups generate a quotient of
St1Cn (R, ∗) (this is proved similarly to Proposition 7.5). Hence relative property (T )
for long and double root subgroups follows directly from Proposition 8.6(a) if n ≥ 3.
Note that the existence of an invertible antisymmetric element µ ∈ Z(R) implies
that St1C2 (R, ∗) ∼= St−1C2 (R, ∗) by Observation 8.4. Thus, relative property (T ) in
the case n = 2 follows from Proposition 8.6(b).
   Finally, we claim that every short root subgroup lies in a bounded product
of fixed conjugates of long and double root subgroups. This follows easily from
relations (E3) and the fact that {r ∈ I : ∃t ∈ R, rr∗ = t + t∗ } is finitely generated
as a left ideal.                                                                      

  The result of Proposition 8.8 is not entirely satisfactory since its hypotheses
may be hard to verify in specific examples. Things becomes much easier under the
additional assumption that R contains a central element a such that a + a∗ = 1:
Lemma 8.9. Assume that there exists a ∈ Z(R) such that a+a∗ = 1. The following
hold:
    (1) Sym(R) = Symmin (R) and Asym(R) = Asymmin (R).
    (2) P (R, ∗, I) = {(r, rar∗ + t) : r ∈ I, t ∈ Asym(R)}. In particular, the set
        {r ∈ I : ∃t ∈ R, rr∗ = t + t∗ } is equal to I.
Proof. (1) For any x ∈ Sym(R) we have x = xa + xa∗ = xa + x∗ a∗ = xa +
(xa)∗ ∈ Symmin (R) where the last equality holds since a is central. The equality
Asym(R) = Asymmin (R) is proved similarly.
  (2) By direct computation, any element of the form (r, rar∗ + t) with r ∈ I, t ∈
Asym(R), lies in P (R, ∗, I). Conversely, given (r, u) ∈ P (R, ∗, I), we can write
u = rar∗ + t for some t, and then we must have t + t∗ = 0.                       

     Thanks to this lemma, we obtain the following variation of Proposition 8.8:
Proposition 8.10. Let R be a finitely generated ring with involution ∗. Assume
that there exists a ∈ Z(R) such that a + a∗ = 1, and let I be a finitely generated
left ideal of R. The following hold:
     (a) The group StBCn (R, ∗, I) has property (T ) for any n ≥ 3.
              PROPERTY (T ) FOR GROUPS GRADED BY ROOT SYSTEMS                        65


   (b) Assume in addition that there exists an invertible antisymmetric element
       µ ∈ Z(R), and R is a finitely generated module over a ring generated by a
       finite set of symmetric elements. Then StBC2 (R, ∗, I) has property (T ).


8.4. Twisted groups of types 2 Dn and 2,2 A2n−1 . Recall that the next family
on our agenda were the Steinberg covers of the twisted Chevalley groups of type
2
  Dn (n ≥ 4). These groups can be constructed using our general twisting procedure
by taking G = StDn (R), where R is a commutative ring endowed with involution
σ and Q ⊆ Aut(G) the subgroup of order 2 generated by Dynσ , the composition
of the ring automorphism ϕσ and the Dynkin involution of Dn . However, we shall
present a more general construction, making use of Observation 8.3(2).
   Recall that the Steinberg group StDn (R), for R commutative, was realized as
the group St1Cn (R, ∗, {0}) where ∗ is the trivial involution. It turns out that if we
start with any ring R (not necessarily commutative) endowed with an involution ∗
and an automorphism σ of order ≤ 2 which commutes with ∗, then the analogous
twisting on St1Cn (R, ∗) can be constructed.
   Example 3: Steinberg groups St1BCn (R, ∗, σ). Let R be a ring endowed with an
involution ∗ and an automorphism σ of order ≤ 2 which commutes with ∗. The
fixed subring of σ will be denoted by Rσ . In this example we will construct the
group St1BCn (R, ∗, σ) graded by the root system BCn .
   Let Φ = Cn+1 and G = St1Cn+1 (R, ∗), the group constructed in Example 1 with
ω = 1. Denote the roots of Φ by ±εi ± εj and ±2εi , and let {Zγ }γ∈Φ be the grading
of G constructed in Example 1.
   Let ρ be the automorphism of ⊕n+1   i=1 Rεi given by
                    ρ(εi ) = εi for 1 ≤ i ≤ n and ρ(εn+1 ) = −εn+1 .
Clearly ρ stabilizes Φ. We claim that there exists an automorphism q = qσ ∈
Aut(G) of order 2 such that



(8.4)               q(zγ (r)) = zρ(γ) (±σ(r)) for all γ ∈ Φ, r ∈ R



(for some choice of signs). Unlike Examples 1 and 2, we cannot prove the existence
of such q by referring to general results from § 8.2. One (rather tedious) way to prove
this is first to define q as an automorphism of the free product ?γ∈Φ Zγ (using (8.4)),
and then show that for a suitable choice of signs in (8.4), q respects the defining
relations of G established in Example 1 and hence induces an automorphism of G.
However, we will also give a conceptual argument for the existence of q at the end
of this example.
   Now let η : ⊕n+1              n

   Now assume that R is commutative, so that the identity map id is an involution.
Then J = {0} is a valid form parameter of (R, id, 1), and the double root subgroups
of St1BCn (R, id, σ, I, {0}) are trivial. Hence we obtain a group graded by a system
of type Bn . This group will be denoted by StBn (R, σ, I).
   We let StBn (R, σ) = StBn (R, σ, R) = St1BCn (R, id, σ, R, {0}) This is the Stein-
berg cover for the twisted Chevalley group of type 2 Dn+1 over R, which we discussed
at the beginning of this example.

   We now state a sufficient condition for the groups St1BCn (R, ∗, σ, I, J) to have

Proposition 8.11. Assume that
    (i) Rσ is finitely generated as a ring
   (ii) {r ∈ I : ∃ t ∈ J, t − rσ(r∗ ) ∈ Rσ } is finitely generated as an Rσ -module
  (iii) J ∩ Rσ is finitely generated as a form parameter of (Rσ , ∗, 1).
Then the group St1BCn (R, ∗, σ, I, J) has property (T ) for any n ≥ 3.
Proof. The proof is analogous to that of Proposition 8.8(a).                          
  If R is commutative, involution ∗ is trivial and J = {0}, the set defined in (ii)
above coincides with I (since rσ(r) always lies in Rσ ), and condition (iii) is of
course vacuous. Thus, as a special case of Proposition 8.11, we have the following:
Proposition 8.12. Assume that
    (i) Rσ is finitely generated as a ring
   (ii) I is finitely generated as an Rσ -module
Then the group StBn (R, σ, I) has property (T ) for any n ≥ 3.

   As in Example 2, the hypotheses necessary to prove property (T ) can be simpli-
fied in the presence of a nice element. This time we wish to assume that R contains
a (not necessarily central) element a such that a + σ(a∗ ) = 1.
Lemma 8.13. Let R, ∗, σ, I and J be as above, and suppose that there exists a ∈ R

In particular, the set {r ∈ I : ∃ t ∈ J, t − rσ(r∗ ) ∈ Rσ } (appearing in condition (ii)
of Proposition 8.11) is equal to I.

Another definition of the groups St1BCn (R, ∗, σ). There is a less intuitive, but
in some sense more convenient, way to construct the groups St1BCn (R, ∗, σ). The
construction we described uses the twist by qσ on the group St1Cn+1 (R, ∗) which, in
turn, was itself constructed using the twist by Dyn∗ on StA2n+1 (R). It is easy to
see that St1BCn (R, ∗, σ) can also be obtained directly from StA2n+1 (R) as follows.
   Let π 0 be the permutation (n + 1, n + 2) and τ the automorphism of StA2n+1 (R)
defined by
                           τ (xei −ej (r)) = xeπ0 (i) −eπ0 (j) (σ(r)).
Note that τ commutes with Dyn∗ , and let Q be the group generated by τ and Dyn∗
(so that Q ∼= Z/2Z × Z/2Z). Then St1BCn (R, ∗, σ) can be obtained from StA2n+1 (R)
using the twist by Q. One advantage of this approach is that the existence of
the automorphism qσ defined above follows automatically, without case-by-case
verification.
   Summary of Examples 1-3. For the reader’s convenience below we list all
the twisted Steinberg groups constructed in Examples 1-3, including the key special
cases and relations between them. In all examples, n ≥ 2 is an integer, R is a ring
and ∗ is an involution on R.
68       MIKHAIL ERSHOV, ANDREI JAIKIN-ZAPIRAIN, AND MARTIN KASSABOV


1. The groups Stω   Cn (R, ∗, J) where ω is an element of U (Z(R)) and J is a form
parameter of (R, ∗, ω).
Special cases:
     (i) Stω              ω
           Cn (R, ∗) = StCn (R, ∗, R);
                        −1
    (ii) StCn (R) = StCn (R, id) where R is commutative;
   (iii) StDn (R) = St1Cn (R, id, {0}) where R is commutative.
2. The groups StBCn (R, ∗, I) where I is a left ideal of R.
Special cases:
    (i) St1Cn (R, ∗) = StBCn (R, ∗, {0}).
3. The groups St1BCn (R, ∗, σ, I, J) where σ is an automorphism of order ≤ 2 com-
muting with ∗, I ⊆ R is a left Rσ -submodule and J ⊆ Asym(R) is a form parameter
of (R, ∗, 1).
Special cases:
      (i) StBn (R, σ, I) = St1BCn (R, id, σ, I, {0}) where R is commutative;
     (ii) StBn (R, σ) = StBn (R, σ, R);
    (iii) StBn (R) = StBn (R, id).

and let u be an arbitrary element of R in the case of Zα+β . In the non-twisted case
70       MIKHAIL ERSHOV, ANDREI JAIKIN-ZAPIRAIN, AND MARTIN KASSABOV


there is no problem with Zα+2β either as we can take u = 1 and arbitrary t ∈ R in
(E1) (in general we cannot do this as t must come from Rσ ).
   To check the required property for the subgroup Zα+2β in the general (twisted)
case we need to show that elements of the form uσ(u)t with u ∈ R, t ∈ Rσ span
R. Indeed, denote the span of those elements by M . Then M contains all elements
of Rσ , in particular all elements of the form u + σ(u) + σ 2 (u). It also contains
all elements of the form (u + 1)(σ(u + 1)) − uσ(u) − 1 = u + σ(u). Since u =
u + σ(u) + σ 2 (u) − (σ(u) + σ(σ(u))), we are done with (a).
   We now prove (b). The subgroup of G generated by long root subgroups is
isomorphic to a quotient of St3 (Rσ ) (and Rσ is finitely generated), so condition (b)
for long root subgroups holds by Theorem 7.12. It remains to check (b) for short
root subgroups. We shall show that any short root subgroup lies in a bounded
product of long root subgroups and finite sets. By symmetry, it is enough to
establish this property for Zα+2β . For any set S we put Zα+2β (S) = {zα+2β (s) :
s ∈ S}.
   Put A = {uσ(u) : u ∈ R}. In the proof of (a) we showed that A generates R
as an Rσ -module. Thus by our assumption there is a finite subset U ⊆ A which
generates R as an Rσ -module. Let S be a finite generating set of Rσ .
   Now fix s ∈ S and u ∈ U , and let t ∈ Rσ be arbitrary. Similarly to the
case of non-twisted G2 , if we calculate the quantity [zα (t), zβ (su)][zα (ts), zβ (u)]−1
using relations (E1), we obtain that {zα+2β (t(s2 − s)uσ(u)) : t ∈ Rσ } lies in a
bounded product of long root subgroups and fixed elements of short root subgroups.
                                                                     σ
Similarly, this property holds for the set {z
                                           Pα+2β (2tuσ(u)) : t ∈ R } andPhence also
for the set Zα+2β (IU ) where I = 2Rσ + s∈S (s2 − s)Rσ and IU = { u∈U ru u :
ru ∈ I}.
   As we have already seen (in the case of non-twisted G2 ), I is a finite index ideal
of Rσ whence IU has finite index in Rσ U = R. Hence Zα+2β can be written as a
product of Zα+2β (IU ) and some finite set. This finishes the proof of (b).             

Lemma 8.18. For any r ∈ R the following hold:
   (i) zα+β (r) ∈ G and zβ (r) ∈ G

Proof. Note that it suffices to prove both statements when r is of the form r = ai w,
where w is a monomial in T + . Let us prove that both (i) and (ii) hold for such r
by induction on m = length(w).
  If m ≤ d, then zα+β (r), zβ (r) ∈ S + ⊂ G by definition of S + . Also by (8.5) we
have zα+β (r) = zα+2β (rr∗ )[zα (1), zβ (r)]−1 , so both (i) and (ii) hold.
  Now fix m > d, and assume that for any monomial w0 ∈ T of length less than
m both (i) and (ii) hold for r = ai w0 .
Claim 8.19. Let q be some tail of w with 2 ≤ length(q) ≤ d + 1 so that w = pq
for some p. Then (i) and (ii) hold for r = ai pq = ai w if and only if (i) and (ii)
hold for r = ai pq ∗ .

Remark. Note that if q = ti1 . . . tis , then q ∗ = tis . . . ti1 is the monomial obtained
from q by reversing the order of letters.

Proof. Consider the element v = p(q + q ∗ ). Then

(8.8)         [zα (q + q ∗ ), zβ (ai p)] = zα+β (−ai v)zα+2β (ai p(q + q ∗ )p∗ a∗i ).

Notice that p(q + q ∗ )p∗ = u + u∗ for u = pqp∗ . Furthermore, length(u) ≤ 2m − 2,
so we can write u = w1 w2∗ where w1 and w2 are monomials of length < m . Then

  zα+2β (ai (u + u∗ )a∗i ) = zα+2β (ai w1 (ai w2 )∗ + ai w2 (ai w1 )∗ )

A similar argument shows that zβ (ai pq) ∈ G ⇐⇒ zβ (ai pq ∗ ) ∈ G.                  

   By Claim 8.19, in order to prove that zα+β (ai w), zβ (ai w) ∈ G we are allowed to
replace w by another word obtained by reversing some tail of w of length ≤ d + 1,
and this operation can be applied several times. The corresponding permutations
clearly generate the full symmetric group on d+1 letters, and since T has d elements,
we can assume that w has a repeated letter at the end: w = pt2 where t ∈ T . But
then we have

Proof of Lemma 8.17. By Lemma 8.18, G contains the root subgroups Zβ and
Zα+β . Hence, by relations (8.5), G contains all elements of the form zα+2β (r + r∗ ).
Since by definition G also contains all elements of the form zα+2β (u) with u ∈ U
and U generates J as a form parameter, we conclude that G contains Z = Zα+2β .
This completes the proof of Lemma 8.17(a).
   From relations (8.6) we have Z ⊆ [N, G]Zα+β , which together with Lemma 8.18(ii)
shows that Z ⊆ [N, G]hSα+2β i. This proves the second assertion of Lemma 8.17(b).
Finally, by (8.5), for every r ∈ J we have zα+2β (2r) = [zβ (r), zα+β (1)] ∈ [N, G],
which proves the first assertion of Lemma 8.17(b).                                 

Proposition 8.20. Let R be a finitely generated ring. Then the group St2 F4 (R)
has property (T ).
Proof. As usual, we need to check two things:
   (a) The Ψ-grading of St2 F4 (R) is strong at (γ, B) for each Borel subset B and
       each root γ in the core of B;
   (b) The pair (St2 F4 (R), Zγ ) has relative (T ) for each γ ∈ Ψ.
               PROPERTY (T ) FOR GROUPS GRADED BY ROOT SYSTEMS                             77


As usual, it suffices to check (a) for the Borel set with boundary {γ1,0 , γ2,0 , γ3,3 }.
Relations (E1) imply condition (a) for the long root subgroups (that is, the root
subgroups Zγ3,i with 0 ≤ i ≤ 2). From relations (E2) and (E3) we obtain condition
(a) for the short root subgroups. This also implies condition (a) for the double root
subgroups since they are contained in the short root subgroups.
   Now let us prove (b). Relations (E4) and (E5) imply that the pair
                     (hZγ3,i , Zγ3,i+4 , Zγ2,i+2 , Zγ2,i+3 i, Zγ2,i+2 Zγ2,i+3 )
is a quotient of (St2 (R) n R2 , R2 ). This yields relative property (T ) for the double
root subgroups. It follows from relations (E6) that any long root subgroup lies in a
bounded product of double root subgroups, so relative (T ) also holds for the long
root subgroups.
   It remains to prove relative property (T ) for the short root subgroups. By
symmetry, it suffices to treat the subgroup Zγ1 . Fix s ∈ R, and consider relation
(E3) with r = 0 and t arbitrary. It implies that the set
                           P0 = {z1,2 (t∗ s, 0)z1,1 (ts, 0) : t ∈ R}
lies in a bounded product of the double and long root subgroups. The same holds
for each of the sets P1 = {z1,2 (t∗ , 0)z1,1 (t, 0) : t ∈ R} (setting s = 1 in P0 ),
P2 = {z1,2 (t∗ s∗ , 0)z1,1 (ts, 0) : t ∈ R} (replacing t by ts in P1 ) and P3 = {z1,2 (t∗ (s +
s∗ ), 0)z1,1 (t(s + s∗ ), 0) : t ∈ R} (replacing s by s + s∗ in P0 ). Considering the
product P0 P2 P3 and using relations (E1) with i = 1, we conclude that the set
P = {z1,1 (t(s + s∗ ), 0) : t ∈ R} lies in a bounded product of the double and long
root subgroups (for any fixed s).
    Let I be the ideal of R generated by {s + s∗ : s ∈ R}. Since R is finitely
generated, it is Noetherian, and so I is generated by a finite subset of {s + s∗ : s ∈
R}. Hence, by what we just proved the set {z1,1 (r, 0) : r ∈ I} also lies in a bounded
product of the double and long root subgroups. On the other hand, every element
of the quotient ring R/I is idempotent (since r2 + r = ((r∗ )∗ + r∗ ) + (r∗ + r)),
whence I has finite index in R. Thus, the set {z1,1 (r, 0) : r ∈ R} lies in a bounded
product of the double and long root subgroups and fixed elements of G. Since the
short root subgroup Zγ1 is a product of {z1,1 (r, 0) : r ∈ R} and Zγ2 , we have
proved relative property (T ) for Zγ1 .                                                      

8.8. More groups graded by root systems. The families of groups graded by
root systems which were described in this section can be generalized in several
different ways, and for many of those generalizations one can prove property (T )
by similar methods. However, in the absence of a specific application, it is not clear
which results of this kind are useful and which are not, so we will not try to achieve
the utmost generality in this subsection. Instead we will describe three new families
of groups with property (T ) which will be needed in § 9 where we will prove that
the class of all finite simple groups of Lie type of rank at least 2 admits a mother
group with property (T ).
   All these families are slight variations of Steinberg groups described earlier in
this section and will be defined by the same procedure as, for instance, the groups
Stω                                    ω
  Cn (R, ∗, J) were obtained from StCn (R, ∗), that is, by decreasing (some of) the
root subgroups, so that the decreased subgroups still form a grading (by the same
root system) and then taking the graded cover of the subgroup generated by these
decreased root subgroups. The main difference is that in the previously considered
78       MIKHAIL ERSHOV, ANDREI JAIKIN-ZAPIRAIN, AND MARTIN KASSABOV


examples, how much a root subgroup Xγ was decreased depended only on the root
length of γ, while in the examples in this subsection the procedure will not be
“symmetric”.
   It will be convenient to use the following notation and terminology. Let G be one
of the types of Steinberg groups considered in this section associated to a ring R
endowed with some set of operations, that is, G = StAn (R), G = Stω             Cn (R, ∗), etc.,
and let {Xγ }γ∈Φ be its standard grading. By definition, each root subgroup Xγ is
isomorphic to certain group Rγf ull (which in many cases is defined as a subgroup of
(R, +)), and in the course of our construction we have chosen a specific isomorphism
between Xγ and Rγf ull . Below we recall the explicit description of Rγf ull in the cases
which will be used in this section:
      (i) if G = StAn (R), then Rγf ull = (R, +) for all γ;
     (ii) if G = StωCn (R, ∗), then Rγ
                                      f ull
                                            = (R, +) if γ is short and Rγf ull = (Symω (R), +)
          if γ is long
   (iii) if G = StBCn (R, ∗), then Rγf ull = (R, +) if γ is long, Rγf ull = (Asym(R), +)
          if γ is double, and Rγf ull = P (R, ∗) if γ is short (where P (R, ∗) is defined
          in Example 2),
    (iv) if G = St1BCn (R, ∗, σ), then Rγf ull = (Rσ , +) if γ is long, Rγf ull = (Asym(Rσ ), +)
          if γ is double, and Rγf ull = Q(R, ∗, σ) if γ is short (where Q(R, ∗, σ) is de-
          fined in Example 3)
Definition. In the above setting, choose a subgroup Rγ of Rγf ull for each γ ∈ Φ,
and let Xγ (Rγ ) be the image of Rγ under the chosen isomorphism Rγf ull → Xγ .
We will say that {Rγ } is a root content for G if {Xγ (Rγ )}γ∈Φ is a Φ-grading.
Example 8.21. Let n ≥ 2 be an integer, R a ring, and let G = StAn (R). Choose a
subgroup Rγ of (R, +) for each γ ∈ An . Then {Rγ }γ∈An is a root content for G if
and only if Rα Rβ ⊆ Rα+β whenever α + β is a root.
   If {Rγ } is a root content for G, we can consider the subgroup h∪Xγ (Rγ )i gener-
ated by decreased root subgroups Xγ (Rγ ) and take its graded cover. This graded
cover will be denoted by G({Rγ }).
Example 8.22. Let G = Stω     Cn (R, ∗), let J be a form parameter of (R, ∗, ω), and
define {Rγ }γ∈Cn by Rγ = R if γ is short and Rγ = J if γ is long. Then {Rγ }γ∈Cn
is a root content for G, and the associated group G({Rγ }) is the group Stω
                                                                          Cn (R, ∗, J)
as defined in Example 2.
   We are now ready to describe the three families we will be interested in. In all
statements below, the following convention will be used: given additive subgroups
A and B of a ring R, by AB we denote the additive subgroup of R generated by
the set {ab : a ∈ A, b ∈ B}.

             9. Application: Mother group with property (T )
    Let Γ be a finite graph and let ε > 0 be a real number. We say that Γ is an
ε-expander if for every subset A consisting of at most half of vertices of Γ we have
|∂A| ≥ ε|A|. Here ∂A is the edge boundary of A, that is, the set of edges of Γ which
join a vertex in A with a vertex outside of A. Expander graphs play an important
role in computer science and combinatorics, and many efforts have been devoted
to their constructions (see, e.g., [HLW]). Particular attention has been paid to the
case of Cayley graphs. Recall that given a group G and a symmetric generating set
S, one defines Cay(G, S) to be the graph with vertex set G, in which two vertices
x and y are connected by an edge if and only if y = xs for some s ∈ S. Note that
if |S| = k then the Cayley graph is k-regular. We will say that an infinite family F
of groups is a family of expanders if there exists k ∈ N and ε > 0 such that every
group G ∈ F has a symmetric generating set S with |S| = k such that Cay(G, S)
is an ε-expander. As a consequence of several works (see [Ka2, KLN, BGT]) the
following remarkable result was recently established.
Theorem 9.1. Any family of (non-abelian) finite simple groups is a family of
expanders.
Definition. Let F be a family of groups and G a group. We say that G is a mother
group for F if every group in F is a quotient of G.
   As discussed in the introduction, one of the conceptually simplest ways to prove
that a family F of finite groups is a family of expanders is to find a mother group
for F with property (T ) or (τ ). Recall that a group G has property (τ ) if there
exists µ > 0 and a finite subset S of G such that κ(G, S, V ) ≥ µ for every non-trivial
irreducible unitary representation V of G which factors through a finite quotient of
G.
   In view of Theorem 9.1, it is natural to ask which families of non-abelian finite
simple groups admit a mother group with (T ) or (τ ).
Conjecture 9.2.
  (a) The family of all non-abelian finite simple groups has a mother group with
      property (τ ).
  (b) A family F of non-abelian finite simple groups has a mother group with
      property (T ) if and only if F contains only finitely many finite simple groups
      of Lie type of rank 1.
84        MIKHAIL ERSHOV, ANDREI JAIKIN-ZAPIRAIN, AND MARTIN KASSABOV


     The main result of this section partially confirms part (b) of Conjecture 9.2:
Theorem 9.3. The family of all finite simple groups of Lie type and rank ≥ 2 has
a mother group with property (T ).
   Before starting the proof of Theorem 9.3, let us mention other known facts
related to Conjecture 9.2. It is a folklore result that a group with property (T )
cannot map onto PSL2 (Fq ) for infinitely many q. Since we are unaware of the proof
of this fact in the literature, we include it at the end of this section. However some
infinite families of finite simple groups of Lie type and rank 1 have a mother group
with property (τ ). For instance, SL2 (Z[1/2]) has property (τ ) (see, e.g. [LZ, p. 60])
and clearly maps onto PSL2 (Fp ) when p is odd.
   With regard to Conjecture 9.2(a), we will also prove that alternating groups
satisfy the conjecture:
Theorem 9.4. The family of all alternating groups Alt(n) has a mother group with
property (τ ).
     Theorem 9.4 will be established in § 9.6.
9.1. Some general reductions. Recall that finite simple groups of Lie type can
be realized as (possibly twisted) Chevalley groups over finite fields. To simplify the
exposition below we shall only discuss groups of rank at least two.
   Given a reduced irreducible classical root system Φ of rank ≥ 2, the (untwisted)
finite simple group of type Φ over the field of order q will be denoted by Φ(q).
Twisted finite simple groups will be denoted by symbols of the form l Φ(q). In all
cases except 2 F4 the parameter q can be any prime power, and the group l Φ(q) is
the set of elements of Φ(q l ) fixed by certain automorphism of order l. The groups
2
  F4 (q) exist only when q = 2m , with m odd, and by definition 2 F4 (q) is the set of
fixed points of an automorphism of order 2 acting on F4 (q). The groups l Φ(q) are
simple with the exception of B2 (2) ∼   = C2 (2), G2 (2) and 2 F4 (2), and in those three
       l
cases Φ(q) contains a simple subgroup of index 2 (which is equal to the commutator
subgroup of l Φ(q)). All these groups are naturally graded by root systems and some
of them are classical groups.
   It will be convenient to use the following terminology: given two groups of Lie
                    0
type l Xm (q) and l X 0 m0 (q 0 ) (where X and X 0 stand for A, B, C, D, E, F or G,
and a non-twisted group Φ(q) is temporarily denoted by 1 Φ(q)), we will say that
                       0
      (i) l Xm (q) and l X 0 m0 (q 0 ) have the same Lie type if X = X 0 , l = l0 and m = m0 ;
                         0
     (ii) l Xm (q) and l X 0 m0 (q 0 ) lie in the same Lie family if X = X 0 , l = l0 and in
          addition m and m0 have the same parity if X = A and l = 2.
In Table 9.1 below we recollect all this information, with groups sorted by their
Lie family (we only list groups of Lie rank ≥ 2). The first column contains the
notation for the group, in the second we put its interpretation as a classical group
(if such exists) and in the third we describe the graded cover of this group as de-
fined in § 7 and § 8. All these correspondences can be found in [Ca] (see Theorems
11.1.2, 11.3.2, 14.4.1, 14.5.1 and 14.5.2). The notation for classical groups is taken
from [KL].
