it suffices to find a mother group with property (T ) for all finite simple groups of
rank ≥ 2 within a given Lie family. For the same reason we can exclude any finite
set of groups from consideration, so we do not have to worry about the commutator
subgroups of C2 (2), G2 (2) and 2 F4 (2) not included in the above table. Further, it
86       MIKHAIL ERSHOV, ANDREI JAIKIN-ZAPIRAIN, AND MARTIN KASSABOV


will be convenient to split all groups in a given Lie family into two subfamilies –
those of rank ≥ c and those of rank < c (but ≥ 2), where c is chosen separately for
each Lie family. We will refer to the corresponding two cases as unbounded rank
case and bounded rank case, and the construction of a mother group with (T ) in
these two cases will be rather different. In the bounded rank case we will treat all
groups of a given Lie type separately (there are finitely many of such Lie types), and
the argument will work for any value of c, while in the unbounded rank case certain
minimum value of c is required (we will often choose not the smallest possible c to
avoid unnecessary technicalities). Note that we need to consider unbounded rank
case only for groups in the families A, B, C, D, 2 A or 2 D.
   The existence of covering epimorphisms from Steinberg groups onto finite simple
groups claimed in the above table is a direct consequence of the construction of
finite simple groups of Lie type as (possibly twisted) Chevalley groups, as defined
in [Ca] and the definition of (possibly twisted) Steinberg groups given in this paper.
The case of the groups of type 2 F4 is somewhat exceptional – in the definition of
finite simple groups of type 2 F4 given in [Ca] the twisting involution on F4 (22k+1 ) is
constructed as a composition of a graph automorphism and a field automorphism,
while in our definition the twisting involution q on Steinberg groups of type F4
is defined directly. The fact that the involution on F4 (22k+1 ) corresponding to q
coincides with the one defined in [Ca] is easy to check by a direct computation;
alternatively, the reader may consult Tits’ paper [Ti], where both definitions are
discussed.
   We now start discussing the proof of Theorem 9.3.
9.2. Bounded rank case. Fix a reduced irreducible classical root system Φ. In
order to establish Theorem 9.3 in the bounded rank case we need to prove the
following:
     (1) (untwisted case) If rk(Φ) ≥ 2, the family of all finite simple groups of the
         form Φ(q) (where q is an arbitrary prime power) admits a mother group
         with (T ).
     (2) (twisted case) If l = 2 or 3 is such that the Lie type l Φ is defined and has
         rank ≥ 2, the family of all finite simple groups of the form l Φ(q) admits a
         mother group with (T ).
We start with the untwisted case where the argument is very simple. According
to our table, it is enough to find a mother group with (T ) for the family StΦ (Fq ).
Since a finite field is generated by one element (as a ring), it is a quotient of Z[t] and
thus StΦ (Fq ) is a quotient of StΦ (Z[t]), which has property (T ) since rk(Φ) ≥ 2.
   Now we turn to twisted groups. Although Z[t] can still be used as the “covering
ring” in many cases, to simplify the arguments, we will use slightly larger rings. In
cases 1-4 below we let R = Z[t1 , t2 ], the ring of polynomials in two (commuting)
variables, and let ∗ : R → R be the involution (also an automorphism of order 2
since R is commutative) which permutes t1 and t2 .
Lemma 9.5. Let S = Sym(R, ∗), the set of elements of R fixed by ∗. The following
hold:
    (1) S = Z[t1 + t2 , t1 t2 ]
    (2) R is a finitely generated left module over S.
    (3) S is generated by 1 as a form parameter of (R, −1).
    (4) Asym(R, ∗) = Asymmin (R, ∗)
               PROPERTY (T ) FOR GROUPS GRADED BY ROOT SYSTEMS                            87


Proof. (1) is, of course, a standard result about symmetric functions, and (2) holds
since R is finitely generated and integral over S.
   We now prove (3). Let J be the form parameter of (R, −1) generated by 1. It
suffices to show that (t1 + t2 )n (t1 t2 )m ∈ J for any n, m > 0. If n = 0 this is clear
                        m ∗
since (t1 t2 )m = tm                                                             n        m
                    1 (t1 ) , and if n > 0, this follows from equality (t1 + t2 ) (t1 t2 ) =
      ∗                         n−1          m
r + r where r = t1 (t1 + t2 )        (t1 t2 ) .
   Finally, to prove (4), note that any r ∈ Asym(R, ∗) cannot contain monomials
of the form ti1 ti2 with nonzero coefficients, and for any i 6= j the coefficients of ti1 tj2
and tj1 ti2 must be opposite, so r = s − s∗ for some s ∈ R.                                 

    Now we begin case-by-case proof.
    Case 1: l Φ = 2 A2n−1 (n ≥ 2). As before, let ¯¯ : Fq2 → Fq2 be the automorphism
of Fq2 of order 2. As we see from the table, 2 A2n−1 (q) is a quotient of St1Cn (Fq2 , ¯¯),
and by Observation 8.4, St1Cn (Fq2 , ¯¯) is isomorphic to St−1     Cn (Fq 2 , ¯¯). Let β be a
generator of Fq , and choose α ∈ Fq2 \ Fq such that α + ᾱ = β. Such α exists since
there are q elements of Fq2 whose trace in Fq is equal to β and not all these elements
lie in Fq . Note that ᾱ = αq , so the subfield generated by α properly contains Fq
and thus must equal Fq2 .
    Now define a homomorphism π : R → Fq2 by setting π(t1 ) = α and π(t2 ) = ᾱ.
By construction, π is involution preserving, that is, π(r∗ ) = π(r) for all r ∈ R,
π is surjective by the choice of α and π(Sym(R, ∗)) = Sym(Fq2 , ¯¯) = Fq by the
choice of β. Therefore, from the definition of Steinberg groups of type Cn , it is
                                                                    −1
clear that π induces an epimorphism from St−1        Cn (R, ∗) to StCn (Fq 2 , ¯¯). The group
St−1
   Cn (R, ∗) has property (T ) by Proposition 8.6 and Lemma 9.5(2)(3), with part
(2) only needed for n = 2. Note that the only reason we had to use isomorphism
between St1Cn (Fq2 , ¯¯) and St−1
                               Cn (Fq 2 , ¯¯) is to cover the case n = 2 since we do not
know any sufficient condition for property (T ) for Steinberg groups of type St1C2 .
   Case 2: l Φ = 2 A2n (n ≥ 2). For n ≥ 3 we can again use the ring R = Z[t1 , t2 ]
with the same involution ∗. Let α be a generator of Fq2 , and define an epimorphism
π : R → Fq2 by setting π(t1 ) = α and π(t2 ) = ᾱ.
   Note that Asym(Fq2 , ¯¯) is precisely the set of elements of Fq2 whose Fq -trace is
equal to zero, and therefore Asym(Fq2 , ¯¯) = Asymmin (Fq2 , ¯¯) by Hilbert’s Theorem
90. Therefore,
                            π(Asym(R, ∗)) = Asym(Fq2 , ¯¯).
Indeed, the left-hand side is clearly contained in the right-hand side, but on the
other hand π(Asym(R, ∗)) ⊇ π(Asymmin (R, ∗)) = Asymmin (Fq2 , ¯¯) = Asym(Fq2 , ¯¯).
This implies that π induces an epimorphism from St1BCn (R, ∗) to St1BCn (Fq2 , ¯¯), and
the group St1BCn (R, ∗) has property (T ) by Proposition 8.8 (note that the hypothe-
sis about finite generation as a left ideal holds automatically since R is Noetherian).
    If n = 2, Proposition 8.8 is not applicable in this setting since R does not have
invertible antisymmetric elements. To fix this problem, we consider the larger ring
R0 = R[s, 1/s], and extend the involution ∗ to R0 by setting s∗ = −s. Extend
the map π : R → Fq2 to an epimorphism π 0 : R0 → Fq2 by sending s to any
nonzero element with zero Fq -trace. By the same argument as above, π 0 induces an
epimorphism form St1BC2 (R0 , ∗) to St1BC2 (Fq2 , ¯¯), and it remains to show that the
pair (R0 , ∗) satisfies the hypotheses of Proposition 8.8 for n = 2.
88        MIKHAIL ERSHOV, ANDREI JAIKIN-ZAPIRAIN, AND MARTIN KASSABOV


    By construction, s is an invertible antisymmetric element of R0 . A straight-
forward computation shows that Asym(R0 , ∗) is generated by s as a form pa-
rameter of (R0 , ∗, −1). Finally, R0 is clearly a finitely generated module over
Z[t1 + t2 , t1 t2 , s2 , 1/s2 ], which is a finitely generated subring of Sym(R0 , ∗) (in fact,
it is easy to show that this subring coincides with Sym(R0 , ∗)).
   Case 3: l Φ = 2 Dn (n ≥ 4). The proof in case is completely analogous to Case 1.
This time we use StBn−1 (R, ∗, R) as a mother group. It has property (T ) by Propo-
sition 8.12.
     Case 4: l Φ = 2 E6 . The proof in this case is also analogous to Case 1.
   Case 5: l Φ = 3 D4 . Here we need a slight modification of the argument in Case 1.
Let R = Z[t1 , t2 , t3 ], and let σ be the automorphism of order 3 which cyclically
permutes t1 , t2 and t3 . As in Lemma 9.5, Rσ = Z[t1 +t2 +t3 , t1 t2 +t1 t3 +t2 t3 , t1 t2 t3 ]
and R is a finitely generated module over Rσ , so the group StG2 (R, σ) has property
(T ) by Proposition 8.14.
   Now let β be a generator of Fq , and choose any α ∈ Fq3 \Fq with α+θ(α)+θ2 (α) =
β. As in Case 1, α generates Fq3 , and the map π : R → Fq3 given by π(ti ) = θi−1 (α)
for i = 1, 2, 3 induces an epimorphism from StG2 (R, σ) to StG2 (Fq3 , θ).
   Case 6: l Φ = 2 F4 . Let R = F2 [t1 , t2 ] and let p : R → R be the homomorphism
                                                                                   k+1
which sends t1 to t2 and t2 to t21 , and define ∗ : F22k+1 → F22k+1 by x∗ = x2 .
Let α be a generator of F22k+1 , and define π : R → F22k+1 by π(t1 ) = α and
            k+1
π(t2 ) = α2 . Then π(p(r)) = r∗ for any r ∈ R, so G = St2 F4 (R, p) surjects onto
St2 F4 (F22k+1 , ∗) which, in turn, surjects onto 2 F4 (22k+1 ) according to our table.
Since G has property (T ) by Proposition 8.20, the proof is complete.

9.3. Unbounded rank case: overview. Now we produce a mother group with
property (T) for finite simple groups of Lie type of sufficiently high rank, considering
separately the families An , Cn , Dn , 2 An (n odd), Bn , 2 An (n even) and 2 Dn (in
this order).
   Our general procedure is as follows. Let Φ = {Φn } be a Lie family that we
are considering. The groups Φn (q) have a description as classical groups. We will
define a Ψ-grading for each Φn (q) with n sufficiently large, where Ψ is a root system
depending only on Φ (and not on n). This grading can be obtained by coarsening
the canonical Φn -grading of Φn (q) using a suitable reduction η : Φn → Ψ, but this
fact will not be essential for the proof. Then we will construct a Ψ-graded group
GΦ which maps onto all Φn (q) with n sufficiently large and use of one of the criteria
from § 8 to prove that GΦ has property (T ). In order to simplify arguments (in
particular, to show that GΦ has property (T )), we will sometimes make Ψ larger
than it could be.
   We start with a brief outline of the proof in the case Φ = {An−1 }, which should
also give the reader some idea on how other cases will be handled. Let us first treat
a special case when for a fixed integer d ≥ 3, we consider only n which are multiples
of d. If n = dk, then as we already observed in the introduction, considering n × n
matrices as d × d block matrices with each block being a k × k matrix, we obtain
a natural isomorphism SLn (Fq ) = ELn (Fq ) ∼   = ELd (Matk (Fq )). It is well known
(see Lemma 9.6 below) that Matk (Fq ) can be generated as a ring by two matrices,
so it is a quotient of Zhx, yi, the free associative ring in two variables. Therefore,
              PROPERTY (T ) FOR GROUPS GRADED BY ROOT SYSTEMS                        89


ELd (Matk (Fq )) is a quotient of ELd (Zhx, yi), and the latter group has property
(T ).
   In general, as long as n ≥ d, we can consider an n × n matrix as a d × d block
matrix, but we can no longer guarantee that blocks will have the same size. We
will use block decompositions with d = 4 in which the first three diagonal blocks
have the same size k and the last diagonal block has size l ≤ k (so that n = 3k + l).
The isomorphism ELdk (Fq ) ∼  = ELd (Matk (Fq )) has natural analogue in this setting,
which yields the corresponding Ad−1 -grading on ELn (Fq ). In fact, this grading is
the precisely the coarsened Ad−1 -grading of ELn (Fq ) corresponding to a suitable
reduction An−1 → Ad−1 .
   The obtained Ad−1 -grading shows that the group SLn (Fq ) = ELn (Fq ) is a quo-
tient of the Steinberg group StA3 (R, M, N ) described in Proposition 8.23 where
R = Matk (Fq ), M = Matk×l (Fq ) and N = Matl×k (Fq ). If R  b is a finitely generated
ring, π : R → R an epimorphism and we choose a left ideal M of R
          b                                                  c     b and a right ideal
N
b of Rb such that π(N  b ) = N and π(Mc ) = M , then it is clear from the definitions
that StA3 (R, M, N ) becomes a quotient of StA3 (R,b Mc, N
                                                         b ). However, to prove that
the latter group has property (T ), we need to know that M     c and N   b are finitely
generated and that M N = R. We do not know how to achieve these conditions if
                      c  b    b
we simply take R b = Zhx, yi (most likely it is impossible), so instead we will make
the ring R a little larger by adding suitable generators and relations.

   Some notations. Before proceeding, we introduce some general notations and
state two results on generation of matrix rings that will be repeatedly used below
without further mention.
   If S is a ring (possibly non-commutative), we denote by Shti the (ring-theoretic)
free product of S with Z[t] and by S[t] the largest quotient of Shti in which t is
central, that is, the ring of polynomials over S in one variable t. For instance,
Zhxi = Z[x] and (Zhxi)hyi = Zhx, yi, the free associative ring in two variables.
   If R is a ring, r an element of R and i, j are positive integers, by (r)i,j we will
denote the matrix whose (i, j)-entry is equal to r and all other entries are equal to
0 – the size of the matrix is not specified in the notation, but will always be clear
90       MIKHAIL ERSHOV, ANDREI JAIKIN-ZAPIRAIN, AND MARTIN KASSABOV


from the context. Using this notation, we put
                                                          k
                                                          X
                            Ei,j = (1)i,j and Idk =             Ei,i .
                                                          i=1

The matrix Idk is, of course, the identity element of Matk (R), but may also be
considered as an element of Mati×j (R) for any i, j ≥ k.
   If (k1 , . . . , kd ) is a sequence of positive integers, by a block matrix of type
(k1 , . . . , kd ), we will mean a d × d block-diagonal matrix whose (i, j)-block is a
ki × kj matrix. Thus, any nP              × n matrix can be considered as a block matrix
of type (k1 , . . . , kd ) whenever         ki = n. For 1 ≤ i ≤ n denote by block(i) the
block

                      P  index    i falls under this decomposition, that is, block(i) = j if

   We will need the following result on generation in matrix rings, which will be
proved at the end of § 9.4.
Lemma 9.6. Let F be a finite field and k an integer. If k ≥ 2, then Matk (F )
can be generated by two symmetric matrices. If |F | = q 2 , σ is the automorphism
of F of order 2 and k ≥ 3, then Matk (F ) can be generated by two hermitian (with
respect to σ) matrices.

9.5. Groups of type A1 . We finish this section by proving that any infinite family of
groups of the form PSL2 (q) cannot have a mother group with property (T ):
Theorem 9.7. Let G be group which maps onto PSL2 (q) for infinitely many q. Then G
does not have property (T).
Proof. By way of contradiction, assume that G has property (T ). Write G as F/K, where
F is a finitely generated free group and let G  e = F/K 2 [K, F ]. Then by Theorem 10.5 G     e also
has property (T ). Since G maps on infinitely many PSL2 (q) and SL2 (q) has no subgroups
of index 2, it is easy to see that G    e maps on infinitely many SL2 (q). Let G  e = hX|Ri be
                                                           i        i
                                                                        
                    e with |X| = n finite. Put Xi =          x 11  x 12
a presentation of G                                                       (i = 1, . . . , n) and let
                                                             xi21 xi22
          i
B = Z[xkl : k, l = 1, 2; l = 1, . . . , n] and A = B/I where I is the ideal of B generated by
                               xi11 xi22 − xi12 xi21 − 1 (i = 1, . . . , n)
and the entries of the following matrices
                                                                      
                                                               1   0
                                   r(X1 , . . . , Xn ) −
                                                               0   1
for each r ∈ R.
    Let Ai = Xi ( mod I) ∈ SL2 (A), and let H be the group generated by {Ai }n     i=1 . It is
easy to check that H is a quotient on G,e but on the other hand H maps onto any quotient
of Ge of the form SL2 (R), with R commutative. In particular, H maps onto infinitely many
SL2 (q).
    Let N be the nilradical of A. We claim that the image of H in SL2 (A/N ) is still
infinite. Indeed, since A is Noetherian, N is nilpotent, so the kernel of the map SL2 (A) →
SL2 (A/N ) is also nilpotent. Thus, if the image of H in SL2 (A/N ) was finite, H would be
virtually nilpotent and could not map onto infinitely many SL2 (q).
    Again since A is Noetherian, it has finitely many minimal prime ideals (whose inter-
section is N ), so there exists a minimal prime ideal P of A such that the image of H in
SL2 (A/P ) is infinite. Now, we can finish the proof using, for instance [GHW, Theorem 4],
which asserts that an infinite subgroup of SL2 (D), with D a commutative domain, has the
Haagerup property and so cannot have property (T ). The obtained contradiction finishes
the proof.                                                                                  

Theorem 9.17. There exists a group Γ with property (τ ) which surjects onto all alter-

to the family of open subgroups. However, since Γ is a frame, every finite index subgroup
of Γ is open, that is, Γ has property (τ ) with respect to all finite index subgroups.   


                        10. Estimating relative Kazhdan constants
   The goal of this section is to prove Theorems 2.7 and 2.8 from § 2. For convenience we
shall use the following terminology and notations:
         • A unitary representation V of a group G will be referred to as a G-space.
         • If U is a subspace of V , by PU we denote the operator of orthogonal projection
           onto U . For any nonzero v ∈ V we set Pv = PCv .
                  PROPERTY (T ) FOR GROUPS GRADED BY ROOT SYSTEMS                                         103


10.1. Hilbert-Schmidt scalar product. Consider the spaceP      HS(V ) of Hilbert-Schmidt
operators on V , i.e., linear operators A : V → V such that i kA(ei )k2 is finite where
{ei } is an orthonormal basis of V . The space HS(V ) is endowed with the Hilbert-Schmidt
scalar product given by                   X
                                 hA, Bi =    hA(ei ), B(ei )i.
                                                       i
By a standard argument this definition does not depend on the choice of {ei }. The
associated norm on HS(V ) will be called the Hilbert-Schmidt norm.
   If V is a unitary representation of a group G then HS(V ) is also a unitary representation
of G – the action of an element g ∈ G on an operator A ∈ HS(V ) is defined by (gA)(v) =
gA(g −1 v). If the element g acts by a scalar on V , for instance if g is in the center of G
and V is an irreducible representation, then g acts trivially on HS(V ).
   For any unit vector v ∈ V the projection Pv : V → V is an element in HS(V ) of norm
1. The map v → Pv does not preserve the scalar product. However, we have the following
explicit formula for hPu , Pv i.
Lemma 10.1. If u and v are unit vectors in a Hilbert space V , then

                                              Appendix A.
    This section contains proofs of Theorem 2.3 and 7.10. Both results are virtually iden-
tical to [Ka1, Cor 1.8] and [Ka1, Cor 1.10], respectively (the only difference is that ELn
is replaced by Stn , which does not affect the argument), but we have chosen to provide
proofs of both results to make this paper essentially self-contained.
    Let R be a finitely generated associative ring (with 1). Recall that we defined the
Steinberg group St2 (R) to be the free product R ? R. Given r ∈ R, the element of St2 (R)
corresponding to r from the first (resp. second) copy of R will be denoted by x12 (r) (resp.
x21 (r)).
    Let {r1 , . . . rk } be a finite generating set of R, and let M = hm1 , . . . , md i be a finitely
generated left R-module. Consider       the action of the group St2 (R) 
                                                                          = R?     R on M 2 where
                                    1 r                                     1 0

                                    0 1                                     r 1

St2 (R) and F2 ⊂ M by
          F1 = {x12 (±ri ), x12 (±1), x21 (±ri ), x21 (±1)}     and       F2 = {(±mi , 0), (0, ±mi )}.
Note that |F1 | ≤ 4(k + 1) and |F2 | ≤ 4d.
Theorem A.1. Let hF1 i be the subgroup of St2 (R) generated by F1 , let Γ = hF1 i n M 2 ,
and let F = F1 ∪ F2 . Then the pair (Γ, M 2 ) has relative property (T ), and moreover
                                                               1
                                        κr (Γ, M 2 ; F ) >            ,
                                                             2K(k, d)
                   √   √
where K(k, d) = 12( k + d + 1). 3
Remark. Theorem A.1 remains true for the semi-direct product M 2 o hF1 i where M is a
right R-module and elements of F1 act on M 2 by right multiplication. This is because such
semi-direct product M 2 o hF1 i is isomorphic to h(F1 )op i n (M 2 )op where (M 2 )op = M 2
considered as a left module over the opposite ring Rop and (F1 )op is the image of F1op in
the Steinberg group St2 (Rop ) under the canonical isomorphism St2 (R) → St2 (Rop ).

Proof. Let V be a unitary representation of Γ, and let v ∈ V be (F, ε)-invariant for
some ε > 0. By definition of the Kazhdan ratio κr (Γ, M 2 ; F ) we need to prove that v
is (M 2 , 2K(k, d)ε)-invariant. Without loss of generality we will assume that v is a unit
vector.
    Let P be the projection valued measure on the dual (M 2 )∗ = M ∗ 2 , coming from the
restriction of the representation V to M 2 . Here M ∗ stands for the Pontryagin dual of
(M, +), the additive group of M , that is, M ∗ = Hom (M, +), S 1 , where S 1 is the unit
circle in the complex plane. The set M ∗ is an abelian group with natural topology. Let
µv be the probability measure on M ∗ 2 , defined by µv (B) = hP(B)v, vi.
    The group St2 (R) has a natural right action on M ∗ 2 , which is dual to the standard
left action of St2 (R) on M 2 and is given by
                 χg ((m, n)) = χ(g(m, n)) for all g ∈ G, χ ∈ M ∗
                                                                          2
                                                                              and (m, n) ∈ M 2 .
The following lemma shows that the measure µv is almost F1 -invariant with respect to
this action.
Lemma A.2. For every measurable set B ⊂ M ∗ 2 and every g ∈ F1 we have

   The rest of the proof shows that any probability measure on M satisfying the above

   First we consider the special case R = M = Z, so that we can identify M ∗ with S 1 and

version of this fact.

measure on T2 obtained from µ by pullback via πs .

πs is invariant under the dual (right) action of SL2 (Z) on M ∗ 2 , and therefore we have a

   Using a similar method we can prove the higher-dimensional analog of Theorem A.1:
Theorem A.8. Let R and M be as in Theorem A.1, with generating set {r1 , . . . , rk }
and {m1 , . . . , md }, respectively. Let Γ = Stp (R) n M p for some p ≥ 3, let F1 be the
generating set of Stp (R) consisting of the elements of root subgroups of the form xij (±rl )
and xij (±1), and let F2 be the set of standard generators of M p and their inverses (so
that |F1 | ≤ 2p(p − 1)(k + 1) and |F2 | ≤ 2pd), and let F = F1 ∪ F2 . Then the pair (Γ, M p )
has relative property (T ) and
                                                         1
                                 κr (Γ, M p ; F ) >               ,
                                                      2K(k, d, p)
                      √   √   √
where K(k, d, p) = 20( k + d + p).
Remark. In the above theorem one can replace the group Stp (R) by the free product of
p(p − 1) copies of the additive group of R. Also note that Stp (R) = hF1 i since p ≥ 3.

Proof. The proof is similar to that of Theorem A.1. We start with a unitary representation
V of Γ = Stp (R) n M p and an (F, ε)-invariant unit vector v ∈ V . Let µv be the measure
on M ∗ p coming from the restriction of the representation to the abelian group M p . As
in the case p = 2, the measure µv is almost invariant under the action of the generators
of Stp (R).
   We will write an element χ ∈ M ∗ p as (χ1 , . . . , χp ), where χi ∈ M ∗ . Define the Borel
subsets Bi , Ci of Mk∗ p for 2 ≤ i ≤ p by

                   Ci = {χ ∈ M ∗ p : χ1 = χi 6= 0, χj = 0 for 1 < j < i}.

Using the restriction M ∗ p → M ∗ 2 coming form the inclusion M 2 ⊂ M p and Lemma A.6,
it is easy to see that

   The above argument can be generalized even further. Let N be an R-bimodule gen-

p × q matrices over N and let Stp (R) (resp. Stq (R)) act by left multiplication (resp. right
multiplication). Somewhat informally, we can think of Γ as the “block upper-triangular”

                                                           .

generators of R and N off the diagonal.
